Ph.D. Thesis
Jingyi Sun
Enhancing Understanding of
Language Models
with Varied Explanations
This thesis has been submitted to the Ph.D. School of The Faculty of Science, the
University of Copenhagen on July 31st, 2026.
Advisors: Prof. Isabelle Augenstein and Dr. Pepa Atanasova
arXiv:2611.25314v1  [cs.CL]  19 Nov 2026


“不识庐山真面目，
只缘身在此山中。”
— 苏轼
“The true nature of things reveals itself
only to those who seek
more than a single view.”
— Su Shi
i


Abstract
Language models are increasingly used to support human judgement in com-
plex reasoning tasks, yet their predictions often remain difficult to interpret
and validate. Explainability techniques aim to provide users with insight into
model decision-making, helping expose errors, failure modes, and potentially
biased behaviour while giving stakeholders such as end users a basis for decid-
ing whether and how to rely on model outputs. However, explanations are,
by nature, simplified and potentially imprecise approximations of a language
model’s internal workings. The goal is therefore to obtain explanations that
reflect the underlying computation as closely as possible (faithfulness), appear
understandable to users (plausibility), and help them achieve their intended
goals (utility). Accordingly, evaluation protocols are essential for assessing
explanation quality across all these dimensions.
The complexity of language models, their decision processes, and the
textual inputs they must interpret has motivated diverse forms of explanations,
including the three studied in this thesis: highlight explanations, natural
language explanations, and chain-of-thought reasoning. These forms offer
complementary perspectives on model behaviour and exhibit distinct strengths
and limitations across tasks. At the same time, producing explanations that
satisfy multiple quality dimensions remains challenging, as does designing
reliable methods for evaluating these dimensions across explanation forms
and application settings.
This thesis presents a body of research that collectively aims to improve
people’s understanding of models’ decision-making processes in complex
reasoning tasks. First, it contributes to the area of explainability diagnostics
by proposing two evaluation frameworks for assessing the quality of highlight
explanations across multiple properties and diverse highlight explanation
types, together with a meta-evaluation framework for examining the reliability
of existing faithfulness metrics for chain-of-thought explanations. Second, in
the area of improving the transparency of language models, this thesis proposes
two explainability techniques that generate natural language explanations
designed to be faithful to the model’s prediction process while remaining
concise, coherent, and helpful for humans in understanding model decisions.
Finally, in the area of human–AI interaction, this thesis presents a user study
that explores the roles of evidence and explanations in helping users validate
model verdicts in a fact-checking setting.
ii


Resumé
Sprogmodeller anvendes i stigende grad til at understøtte menneskelige vur-
deringer i komplekse ræsonneringsopgaver, men deres forudsigelser er ofte
fortsat vanskelige at fortolke og validere. Forklarbarhedsteknikker har til
formål at give brugere indsigt i modellernes beslutningsprocesser og dermed
bidrage til at afdække fejl, svigtmønstre og potentielt biaspræget adfærd,
samtidig med at de giver interessenter såsom slutbrugere et grundlag for at
afgøre, om og hvordan de bør forlade sig på modellernes output. Forklaringer
er imidlertid af natur forenklede og potentielt upræcise tilnærmelser til en
sprogmodels interne funktionsmåde. Målet er derfor at opnå forklaringer,
der afspejler den underliggende beregning så præcist som muligt (trofasthed;
faithfulness), fremstår forståelige for brugerne (plausibilitet; plausibility) og
hjælper dem med at nå deres tilsigtede mål (nytteværdi; utility). Evaluer-
ingsprotokoller er derfor afgørende for at kunne vurdere forklaringskvaliteten
på tværs af alle disse dimensioner.
Kompleksiteten i sprogmodeller, deres beslutningsprocesser og de tekstlige
input, som de skal fortolke, har motiveret udviklingen af forskellige forklar-
ingsformer, herunder de tre, der undersøges i denne afhandling: fremhævelses-
baserede forklaringer (highlight explanations), forklaringer i naturligt sprog
(natural language explanations) og ræsonnementskæder (chain-of-thought
reasoning). Disse forklaringsformer giver komplementære perspektiver på
modeladfærd og har forskellige styrker og begrænsninger på tværs af opgaver.
Samtidig er det fortsat udfordrende både at frembringe forklaringer, der
opfylder flere kvalitetsdimensioner, og at udvikle pålidelige metoder til at
evaluere disse dimensioner på tværs af forklaringsformer og anvendelsessam-
menhænge.
Denne afhandling præsenterer en række forskningsbidrag, der samlet har
til formål at forbedre menneskers forståelse af modellers beslutningsprocesser
i komplekse ræsonneringsopgaver. For det første bidrager afhandlingen til
området for diagnostik af forklarbarhed ved at foreslå to evalueringsrammer
til vurdering af kvaliteten af fremhævelsesbaserede forklaringer med hensyn til
flere egenskaber og på tværs af forskellige typer af sådanne forklaringer samt
en meta-evalueringsramme til undersøgelse af pålideligheden af eksisterende
mål for trofasthed i forklaringer baseret på ræsonnementskæder. For det andet
foreslår afhandlingen, som et bidrag til arbejdet med at forbedre sprogmod-
ellers gennemsigtighed, to forklarbarhedsteknikker, der genererer forklaringer i
iii


naturligt sprog, som er udformet med henblik på trofast at afspejle modellens
forudsigelsesproces og samtidig være kortfattede, sammenhængende og nyttige
for mennesker, når de skal forstå modellens beslutninger. Endelig præsenterer
afhandlingen inden for menneske–AI-interaktion en brugerundersøgelse, der
undersøger, hvilken rolle belæg og forklaringer spiller i at hjælpe brugere med
at validere modellens afgørelser i en faktatjekningskontekst.
iv

Part I
Executive Summary
1


Chapter 1
Executive Summary
1.1
Introduction
Language models have demonstrated rapidly improving capabilities across
a wide range of tasks, enabling their use as tools for supporting human
judgement in decision-making settings (Brown et al., 2020; OpenAI, 2023;
Center for AI Safety et al., 2026; McFadyen et al., 2026). However, in decision-
sensitive and high-stakes scenarios, such as fact-checking, users are concerned
not only with the model’s final answer but also with how it is derived, so
that they can decide whether and how to rely on it (Ribeiro et al., 2016;
Bansal et al., 2021; Si et al., 2024). Explainability techniques therefore seek
to bridge opaque model behaviour and users’ practical needs by presenting
a model’s reasoning process in human-interpretable forms. They have been
studied to make model behaviour more inspectable (Ribeiro et al., 2016;
Zhao et al., 2024a), expose potential failure modes (Atanasova et al., 2020a;
Lertvittayakumjorn and Toni, 2021), and calibrate users’ reliance on model
responses (Bansal et al., 2021; Vasconcelos et al., 2023).
Different explanation forms provide complementary perspectives on model
behaviour (see examples in Fig. 1.1). Highlight explanations identify salient
input evidence (Lei et al., 2016; DeYoung et al., 2020), natural language
explanations provide coherent free-text rationales (Camburu et al., 2018;
Atanasova et al., 2023), and chain-of-thought reasoning elicits step-by-step
reasoning traces before the model’s prediction (Wei et al., 2022; Kojima et al.,
2022). Besides, these different forms exhibit distinct limitations and serve
different user needs and application scenarios (Doshi-Velez and Kim, 2017;
2


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
NSYNC was a vocal group that formed in 1998.
NSYNC was an American boy band formed in Orlando in 1995. 
Claim
Evidence
First, the claim makes two
assertions: NSYNC was a vocal
group, and it formed in 1998.
Then, the evidence describes NSYNC
as an American boy band, which
supports the first assertion.
However, the evidence states that
NSYNC formed in 1995,
contradicting the claimed year of
1998.
Finally, because one component of
the conjunctive claim is false, the
overall claim is refuted.
The claim is refuted because, although NSYNC was a
vocal group, the evidence states that it formed in 1995
rather than 1998.
(3) Chain-of-Thought Reasoning
(2) Natural Language Explanation
(1) Highlight Explanations
Figure 1.1: Illustration of three explanation forms for the same FEVER
fact-checking instance (Thorne et al., 2018): Highlight Explanation, Natural
Language Explanation, and Chain-of-Thought Reasoning.
Lyu et al., 2024; Madsen et al., 2024).
Explanation evaluation plays an essential role in explainability re-
search (Zhao et al., 2024a). Plausibility examines whether an explanation
appears coherent and understandable by humans (Jacovi and Goldberg,
2020; Hase et al., 2020), utility directly measures whether it helps the user
achieve an intended goal, such as making more accurate or appropriately
calibrated decisions (Doshi-Velez and Kim, 2017; Hase and Bansal, 2020).
However, these human-facing properties are insufficient: an explanation may
be readable and persuasive while remaining misaligned with the model’s
underlying decision process, thereby raising concerns about its faithfulness
(Atanasova et al., 2023; Agarwal et al., 2024). Unfaithful explanations may
give users unjustified confidence in model outputs, obscure failure modes,
or mislead users about why a model made a decision (Atanasova et al.,
2023; Agarwal et al., 2024). Therefore, plausibility, utility, and faithfulness
constitute complementary dimensions of explanation quality (Lyu et al., 2024;
Zhao et al., 2024a).
Taken together, explanation forms serve different purposes, and these
quality dimensions are operationalised differently across explanation types and
application settings. This variation suggests that no single explanation form
or evaluation metric is sufficient across all tasks and applications. The thesis
3


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
bridges diverse explainability techniques in two ways: through novel evaluation
benchmarks for characterising them along multiple dimensions across tasks,
and through novel methods that combine their strengths. These contributions
are guided by the principle that explanations should be understandable and
useful to users while also serving as faithful indicators of model behaviour.
This section introduces explainability techniques and their core evaluation
protocols across complex reasoning tasks in the era of language models.
Section 1.2 details how the published papers included in this thesis contribute
to the broader goal of evaluating and improving explanations for language
models. Section 1.3 summarises the contributions of the thesis and provides
an introspective reflection on the limitations of each individual work, pointing
to promising directions for future work.
1.1.1
Explanations in Natural Language Processing
Despite the growing performance of language models on a wide range of tasks,
their underlying reasoning processes remain unclear to users. Explainability
techniques aim to reveal the model’s rationale behind its output by producing
different forms of explanations, depending on the level of model behaviour
being explained and the purpose for which the explanations are used (Madsen
et al., 2022; Lyu et al., 2024; Zhao et al., 2024a). Recent work in explainability
has evolved from highlight explanations, which select salient texts from the
input, (Li et al., 2016; Ribeiro et al., 2016; Atanasova et al., 2020a; DeYoung
et al., 2020), towards natural language explanations or intermediate reasoning
traces that are free-form, structured and reasoning-oriented (Wei et al., 2022;
Turpin et al., 2023; Wang and Atanasova, 2025; Alon et al., 2026).
In the following, I detail three commonly used forms of explanations which
are further researched in this thesis: highlight explanations §1.1.1.1, which
are compact input segments that can serve as interface to convey model’s
prediction signals; natural language explanations §1.1.1.2, which are not
constrained to the input text and more plausible for human understanding;
and chain-of-thought explanations §1.1.1.3, which are usually more structured
and express model’s step-by-step reasoning.
1.1.1.1
Highlight Explanations
Highlight explanations, also called input feature explanations (Sun et al.,
2025a), can identify important parts of the input text that are salient for
4


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
The [Rodney King riots] took place in [the most populous county] in the USA.
The 1992 Los Angeles riots, known as the [Rodney King riots], occurred in [Los Angeles County], California.
Claim
Evidence
Highlight Token Explanations
Prediction
Supports
Token Interactive Explanations
Span Interactive Explanations
[         ]
Figure 1.2: Illustration of three types of Highlight Explanations for the same
FEVER fact-checking instance (Thorne et al., 2018): Highlight Token Expla-
nations, Token Interactive Explanations, and Span interactive explanations.
the model’s prediction (Li et al., 2016; DeYoung et al., 2020; Jacovi and
Goldberg, 2021).
By visually linking a prediction to specific important
parts of the input, they provide an intuitive way to communicate model
behaviour to users (Jacovi and Goldberg, 2021). Highlight explanations are
typically extracted using post-hoc explainability techniques and can therefore
be applied broadly without modifying or retraining the predictive model.
Based on the granularity and structure of the highlighted information, they
can be categorised into three major types: highlight token explanations,
token interactive explanations, and span interactive explanations (See the
illustrative examples in Fig. 1.2).
Highlight Token Explanations. The most common form of highlight
explanation assigns an importance score to each input token.
a = (a1, . . . , an),
ai ∈R,
(1.1)
where ai denotes the estimated importance of input token xi to the model’s
prediction ˆy, and n is the input length.
These importance scores can be estimated using several families of ex-
plainability techniques. Perturbation-based methods delete, mask, or replace
individual input tokens and measure the resulting change in model out-
put as their estimated importance (Li et al., 2016; DeYoung et al., 2020).
Gradient-based methods derive attributions from gradients with respect to
input embeddings or intermediate representations (Li et al., 2016; Sundarara-
5


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
jan et al., 2017). Local surrogate methods, such as LIME, approximate the
model’s local behaviour with a simple interpretable model (Ribeiro et al.,
2016). Shapley methods estimate each token’s marginal contributions across
coalitions of input tokens (Shapley, 1953; Lundberg and Lee, 2017). Atten-
tion weights have also been used for token attribution (Jain and Wallace,
2019; Serrano and Smith, 2019; Wiegreffe and Pinter, 2019). These methods
have recently been adapted to decoder-only language models and multi-token
outputs (Sarti et al., 2023; Pan et al., 2026; Pramanik et al., 2026).
In the running example, the most important highlight tokens could be
“Rodney”, “California”, and “USA”. However, these individual tokens cannot
capture interactions that jointly influence the prediction, such as the support-
ing correspondence between “Rodney” in the claim and its occurrence in the
evidence (Chen et al., 2020; Sikdar et al., 2021). This limitation motivates
interactive explanations which attribute importance to pairs or groups of
tokens across input parts.
Token Interactive Explanations.
Token interactive explanations repre-
sent pairwise token interactions as a score matrix:
A = (aij)n
i,j=1,
aij ∈R,
(1.2)
where aij denotes the estimated contribution of the interaction between token
xi and token xj.
Gradient-based methods estimate these effects from second-order or path-
integrated interaction between token embeddings, as in Integrated Hessians
or Integrated Directional Gradients (Tsang et al., 2020; Janizek et al., 2021;
Sikdar et al., 2021). Subsequent work models these interactions as asymmetric
relations, as the contribution of xj conditioned on xi may differ from the
contribution of xi conditioned on xj (Lu et al., 2023).
In the running example, the most important pairs might include
⟨“Rodney”, “Rodney”⟩and ⟨“USA”, “California”⟩. Although these explana-
tions can capture interactions across different parts of the input, such isolated
token pairs may lack semantic coherence. This motivates span interaction
explanations, which capture interactions between multi-token spans rather
than individual tokens.
Span Interactive Explanations.
Span interactive explanations consist of
pairs of contiguous text spans drawn from different parts of the input:
6


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
S = {(sp, sq, wpq)}(p,q)∈I,
(1.3)
where sp and sq denote the paired spans and wpq denotes the estimated
interaction importance, and I indexes the extracted span pairs.
Ray Choudhury et al. (2023) construct a token-level interaction graph over
the input and apply Louvain community detection to identify important span
pairs. The resulting span interactions resemble human annotations, suggesting
they can be useful in the complex reasoning tasks where interactions of input
parts are important for the model.
In the above running example, the most salient span interactive ex-
planations might include ⟨“Rodney King riots”, “Rodney King riots”⟩and
⟨“the most populous county in the USA”, “Los Angeles County”⟩.
By con-
necting semantically coherent phrases across the input parts, they represent
model’s reasoning more naturally than isolated token pairs.
Building on these explainability techniques, this thesis investigates the
evaluation of highlight explanation. Chapter 2 introduces a framework for
comparing these three forms of highlight explanation across multiple properties
in complex reasoning tasks. Chapter 3 focuses on highlight token explanations
and proposes a novel framework for assessing their utility in indicating model
context utilisation in synthetic retrieval-augmented generation settings.
1.1.1.2
Natural Language Explanations
Natural language explanations (NLEs) are free-text rationales for a model’s
prediction that may contain information beyond the input text (Miller, 2019;
Camburu et al., 2018). Their natural language form can make model predic-
tion more accessible to users by providing justifications in natural language
inference and commonsense question answering (Camburu et al., 2018; Yuan
et al., 2025), explaining how the model draws on the evidence to verify
the claim in fact-checking (Atanasova et al., 2020b; Sun et al., 2026c), and
communicate model uncertainty (Yona et al., 2024; Liu et al., 2025a).
NLE systems differ in the dependency between answer and explanation
generation. Following Lyu et al. (2024), they can be broadly grouped into
predict-then-explain, explain-then-predict, and jointly-predict-and-explain ar-
chitectures. The first two orderings were explored in early NLE generation
works, while the last category includes self-rationalisation models that gen-
erate task predictions and rationales within a shared framework (Marasovic
et al., 2022).
7


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
In a predict-then-explain architecture (Camburu et al., 2018; Rajani et al.,
2019), the model first predicts an answer ˆy from the input x and then generates
an explanation z = (z1, . . . , zm) conditioned on both:
ˆy ∼pθy(ˆy | x),
z ∼pθz(z | x, ˆy).
(1.4)
In the reverse explain-then-predict ordering (Camburu et al., 2018; Kumar
and Talukdar, 2020), an explanation is generated before being used to infer
the label:
z ∼pθz(z | x),
ˆy ∼pθy(ˆy | x, z).
(1.5)
A self-rationalisation framework couples answer and explanation learning
within a shared architecture (Atanasova et al., 2020b; Majumder et al., 2022),
in which case predictions are generated together with NLEs.
(z, ˆy) ∼pθ(z, ˆy | x),
(1.6)
Such coupling can improve answer–explanation consistency (Kumar and
Talukdar, 2020; Majumder et al., 2022).
NLE quality can be characterised along two broad dimensions: faithfulness
to the model’s underlying reasoning and human-facing qualities such as
plausibility and utility. These dimensions are discussed in detail in Sec. 1.1.2.2
and Sec. 1.1.3.
Chapters 4 and 5 develop self-rationalisation frameworks designed to
improve faithfulness while preserving human-facing quality. Because highlight
explanations provide compact cues about the input features associated with
the model’s prediction, they can serve as a bridge between model behaviour
and NLEs. Based on these cues, Chapter 4 encodes the highlights in a graph
structure and fine-tunes the model to generate more faithful NLEs; Chapter
5 uses these highlights at inference time to guide the model in generating
rationales that explain its uncertainty by explicitly referring to agreements
and conflicts in the input.
1.1.1.3
Chain-of-Thought Reasoning
Although also in free-text form, chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning was in-
troduced primarily to improve model performance by eliciting “step-by-step”
reasoning before the final answer (Wei et al., 2022; Kojima et al., 2022). CoT
prompting has substantially improved performance on arithmetic, common-
sense, symbolic, and multi-step reasoning tasks (Wei et al., 2022; Kojima
et al., 2022; Zhou et al., 2023).
8


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
As in self-rationalisation, CoT generation is coupled with answer genera-
tion. Given an input x, the model generates a reasoning trace r = (r1, . . . , rT)
comprising intermediate steps towards the final answer ˆy:
r ∼pθ(r | x),
ˆy ∼pθ(ˆy | x, r),
(1.7)
Early work primarily elicited CoT through prompting (Wei et al., 2022;
Kojima et al., 2022; Zhou et al., 2023), and the resulting CoTs can expose
structured natural language traces that users can inspect.
CoT quality can be evaluated along several dimensions, including cor-
rectness, human-facing reasoning quality, and others. However, a coherent
and plausible reasoning trace may still rationalise an answer post hoc rather
than faithfully reflect the factors that influenced the prediction (Turpin et al.,
2023; Lanham et al., 2023; Nguyen et al., 2024). For CoT to serve as a reli-
able explainability tool, its faithfulness must therefore be carefully assessed.
Sec. 1.1.2.3 provides a detailed discussion of CoT faithfulness evaluation and
meta-evaluation.
Chapter 6 proposes a meta-evaluation framework for studying existing
CoT faithfulness metrics. It examines whether optimising a model against one
metric yields improvements under other faithfulness metrics and evaluation
paradigms, thereby testing whether these metrics capture a shared notion of
faithfulness or distinct properties.
1.1.2
Evaluating Faithfulness of Explanations
Faithfulness concerns how accurately an explanation reflects the model’s
underlying reasoning process (Jacovi and Goldberg, 2020; Lyu et al., 2024). It
is distinct from plausibility: an explanation can be fluent, coherent, and per-
suasive to humans while still failing to capture the intermediate computations
that influenced the model’s output (Turpin et al., 2023; Parcalabescu and
Frank, 2024). This distinction is especially important for language models,
whose persuasive but unfaithful explanations can obscure spurious corre-
lations, unsupported evidence use, or other model failures, particularly in
high-stakes settings (Rudin, 2019; Atanasova et al., 2023; Sun et al., 2026b).
Because the model’s decision-making process is not directly observable,
faithfulness is assessed through different proxies that rely on different assump-
tions and might capture different aspects of faithfulness (Atanasova et al.,
2023; Parcalabescu and Frank, 2024; Tutek et al., 2025). Their faithfulness
9


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
conclusions can therefore vary across tasks and models, leaving Faithfulness
an open validation problem (Zaman and Srivastava, 2025; Sun et al., 2026b).
Faithfulness is a core theme of this thesis. Across the contribution chapters,
all except Chapter 7 develop or apply methods for evaluating the faithful-
ness of a specific explanation type, while Chapter 4 and Chapter 5 also
develop methods intended to improve it. The following subsections introduce
common approaches to assessing and improving the faithfulness of highlight
explanations, natural language explanations, and chain-of-thought reasoning.
1.1.2.1
Faithfulness of Highlight Explanations
Faithfulness of highlight explanations is commonly operationalised through
behavioural intervention tests, particularly comprehensiveness and suffi-
ciency (Yu et al., 2019; DeYoung et al., 2020). Comprehensiveness examines
whether ablating the highlight tokens can reduce the model’s confidence in its
original prediction, showing the explanation is not redundant for the model’s
answer; sufficiency tests whether only retaining the explanation text preserves
the prediction, indicating it is sufficient for model’s answer (DeYoung et al.,
2020). Subsequent work has refined these tests by perturbing token repre-
sentations to reduce unnatural inputs (Zhao and Aletras, 2023), aggregating
intervention effects using area over the perturbation curve (AOPC) (Chen
et al., 2021; Ray Choudhury et al., 2023), and normalising AOPC to enable
cross-model and cross-instance comparisons (Edin et al., 2025). Nevertheless,
this line of approach has limitations: interventions may shift the input distri-
bution, remove useful context, or test only one kind of dependence between
the explanation and the prediction (Jacovi and Goldberg, 2020; Lyu et al.,
2024). Therefore, they should be regarded as specific diagnostic tests and
considered alongside other explanation properties.
Chapter 2 compares three forms of highlight explanations across several
diagnostic properties and reveals a tension between faithfulness and agree-
ment with human annotations, particularly for span interactive explanations.
Chapter 3 focuses on highlight tokens and finds they are not necessarily
effective indicators of model context utilisation despite their high faithful-
ness. Together, these findings highlight the importance of scenario-specific
evaluation across multiple explanation properties.
10


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
1.1.2.2
Faithfulness of Natural Language Explanations
NLE faithfulness evaluation.
The free-text form of NLEs complicates
their faithfulness assessment: unlike highlight explanations, they cannot be
directly removed from the input to test the effects on the model’s prediction.
A widely used approach is the counterfactual unfaithfulness test (Atanasova
et al., 2023): when a perturbation on the input, e.g., inserting a word, changes
the model’s prediction, a faithful explanation should mention the inserted
word responsible for that change. Recent works extend this test by considering
shifts in answer probability distribution rather than answer flips (Siegel et al.,
2024), applying concept-level interventions (Matton et al., 2025), or adapting
it to closed-source LMs without access to answer probabilities (Siegel et al.,
2025).
However, these methods are argued to only measure model’s self-
consistency, as they do not assess whether the same internal computations are
used for NLE and answer generation (Parcalabescu and Frank, 2024). Alter-
native approaches therefore assess whether the input attributions underlying
answer and explanation generation aligns (Parcalabescu and Frank, 2024), or
intervene on hidden states and concepts to compare their causal effects on
these two outputs (Yeo et al., 2025; Bhan et al., 2026). Nevertheless, these
methods usually depend on assumptions about which hidden states, concepts,
or internal representations are relevant. More importantly, since there is
no observable ground truth for model’s reasoning, it still remains unclear
whether current metrics measure faithfulness, output-level self-consistency, or
narrower diagnostic properties (Parcalabescu and Frank, 2024; Lyu et al.,
2024; Zaman and Srivastava, 2025).
NLE faithfulness improvement.
Methods for improving NLE faithful-
ness primarily focus on strengthening the alignment between explanation
and answer generation. Early works seek to improve this alignment through
task-specific optimisation objectives (Atanasova et al., 2020b), grounding
NLEs in background knowledge (Majumder et al., 2022), and directly opti-
mising multiple diagnostic properties (Atanasova et al., 2022). More recent
approaches utilise self-refinement in which models revise their explanations
using self-critique, attribution feedback, or verdict-anchored signals (Wang
and Atanasova, 2025; Kong et al., 2026). Another line of work leverages pref-
erence optimisation, guiding the model to generate NLE based on contrasts
between faithful and unfaithful explanation signals (Zhao and Daumé III,
11


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
2025; Admoni et al., 2026). However, these methods depend on reliable
feedback or faithfulness labels that are based on specific proxy metrics, and
therefore do not necessarily guarantee alignment with the model’s internal
computations (Admoni et al., 2026).
Chapters 4 and 5 complement the above studies in a model-intrinsic
way for more faithful NLE generation: highlight explanations, which can
faithfully reflect aspects of the model’s internal reasoning, are used as cues
to align NLEs with answer generation. Chapter 4 introduces G-TEx (Yuan
et al., 2025), which injects highlight explanations through a graph neural
network layer to guide NLE generation. This produces more faithful NLEs,
as evaluated using the aforementioned counterfactual unfaithfulness test.
Chapter 5 introduces CLUE (Sun et al., 2026c), which uses span interactions
as cues to steer the model to generate NLEs that explain its uncertainty. I
also propose a novel metric, extending counterfactual evaluation from binary
answer flips to entropy-based shifts in the answer distribution, to measure
the NLE faithfulness with respect to model uncertainty. Related studies
similarly combine this form of guidance with model self-refinement (Wang
and Atanasova, 2025) or inject explanation importance scores directly to
modify attention heads for NLE generation (Alon et al., 2026).
1.1.2.3
Faithfulness of Chain-of-Thought Reasoning
Despite improving model performance on reasoning tasks (Wei et al., 2022;
Kojima et al., 2022; Zhou et al., 2023), chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning may
not faithfully reflect model’s underlying reasoning process (Lanham et al.,
2023; Paul et al., 2024; Arcuschin et al., 2025).
CoT faithfulness evaluation.
Existing evaluation methods can be broadly
grouped into contextual, parametric, and mechanistic approaches. Contextual
faithfulness tests usually intervene on CoT traces and examine whether it
leads to expected model answer change (Lanham et al., 2023; Bentham et al.,
2024). Feature-bias tests inject answer-position or sycophantic cues to the
input and examine whether the CoT acknowledges them when they change
the answer (Turpin et al., 2023; Chen et al., 2025b). However, subsequent
work fails to verbalise that an influential cue may reflect an incomplete trace
due to limited computation budget rather than unfaithfulness (Zaman and
Srivastava, 2026).
12


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
Since contextual interventions may not necessarily remove information
from the model’s parameters, Tutek et al. (2025) propose to measure paramet-
ric faithfulness by unlearning knowledge associated with individual reasoning
steps and observing the resulting model answer change. However, recent
work finds that unlearning irrelevant or shuffled content can also substantially
affect the resulting scores, suggesting that this measure might be confounded
by unlearning artefacts (Mitsuhashi et al., 2026).
Mechanistic approaches instead compare the CoT with model’s internal
computation, examining whether its reasoning content is reflected in internal
features (Chen et al., 2026; Shiromani et al., 2026), concept directions (Li
et al., 2025b), activation dynamics (Tanneru et al., 2024; Chen et al., 2026),
or circuits (Shen et al., 2026). However, correspondence with selected internal
signals may capture only part of the model’s computation and does not by
itself provide a complete causal account of its reasoning process (Bereska and
Gavves, 2024; Hu et al., 2026; Gao et al., 2026).
Meta-evaluation.
Recent studies have begun to meta-evaluate these faith-
fulness metrics by creating expert-annotated faithful and unfaithful CoT
traces (Shen et al., 2025), or constructing tasks where the model’s output
can reveal which intermediate computations must have occurred (Gur-Arieh
et al., 2026). Although these works provide important validation settings,
their notions of “ground-truth” faithfulness remain tied to particular tasks
and constructions, limiting their generalisation across tasks, models, and
explanation settings.
CoT faithfulness improvement.
Recent work has sought to improve
CoT faithfulness by strengthening the causal role of reasoning traces in
answer generation (Paul et al., 2024; Jia et al., 2026), optimising their sim-
ulatability (Hase and Potts, 2026; Sivakumaran et al., 2026), or deriving
training signals from counterfactual interventions (Swaroop et al., 2025; Akter
et al., 2025). However, these approaches optimise particular proxy notions
of faithfulness, and their gains may not transfer across metrics or evaluation
paradigms (Zaman and Srivastava, 2025; Sun et al., 2026b).
Chapter 6 focuses on meta-evaluating existing CoT faithfulness metrics,
and proposes FaithMATE (Sun et al., 2026b), a unified preference-alignment
framework for optimising either contextual or parametric faithfulness objec-
tives. Although FaithMATE performs optimisation, it is intended to be an
13


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
interface to investigate whether improvements obtained by optimising a model
towards one faithfulness metric transfer to other metrics and paradigms. I
find that contextual and parametric faithfulness are related but asymmetric.
Moreover, gains under one contextual metric do not consistently transfer to
others. These findings suggest that existing metrics capture distinct facets of
faithfulness rather than a single notion and should be evaluated as a family
rather than treated as interchangeable properties.
1.1.3
Evaluating Plausibility and Utility of Explana-
tions
Faithfulness examines whether explanations reflect a model’s underlying
reasoning process, whereas plausibility and utility capture human-facing
properties. Plausibility checks whether explanations appear understandable,
coherent, and convincing, while utility concerns whether they can help users
comprehend the model’s prediction and achieve a concrete goal (Doshi-Velez
and Kim, 2017; Jacovi and Goldberg, 2020; DeYoung et al., 2020). These
properties are commonly assessed through human ratings of explanation
quality (Atanasova et al., 2020b; Wang and Atanasova, 2025; Chuang et al.,
2026), task-studies of how explanations support user decisions (Hase and
Bansal, 2020; Buçinca et al., 2020; Hashemi Chaleshtori et al., 2024), or
comparisons with human-written explanations (Rajani et al., 2019; Aggarwal
et al., 2021).
Manual evaluation of highlight explanations.
Agreement with
human-annotated highlights is commonly used as a proxy for plausibility,
measuring whether explanations resemble those that human annotators con-
sider relevant to a decision (DeYoung et al., 2020). However, later work argues
that the assessment of explanations should cover multiple aspects, but usually
focuses on a single form of highlight explanations (Atanasova et al., 2020a;
Hashemi Chaleshtori et al., 2024). Chapter 2 contributes to this direction by
comparing three types of highlight explanations across multiple dimensions,
including agreement with human annotations, faithfulness, etc. Although
span interactions perform best on most properties, they exhibit a trade-off
between comprehensiveness, one aspect of faithfulness, and agreement with
human annotations. This reinforces that faithfulness and plausibility should
be treated as distinct properties and evaluated separately (DeYoung et al.,
2020; Jacovi and Goldberg, 2020; Atanasova et al., 2020a).
14


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
Manual evaluation of natural language explanations.
Human
evaluation of NLEs commonly assesses fluency, clarity, coherence, informa-
tiveness, factual correctness, redundancy, and helpfulness (Atanasova et al.,
2020b; Wang and Atanasova, 2025; Chuang et al., 2026; Liu et al., 2025a).
Because human evaluation requires substantial annotator time and financial
resources, it is often conducted on a small subset of generated NLEs as a
quality check (Wang et al., 2025; Wang and Atanasova, 2025; Zhao and
Daumé III, 2025). Chapters 4 and 5 similarly conduct human evaluation
to verify that improvements in faithfulness do not compromise plausibil-
ity. Both chapters guide NLE generation using input highlights: G-Tex
encodes these cues through graph-layer and model finetuning (Yuan et al.,
2025), whereas CLUE leverages them via inference-time model steering (Sun
et al., 2026c). The latter responds to fact-checkers’ need for explanations
by explicitly referring to the input conflicts and agreements relevant to the
model’s uncertainty (Warren et al., 2025). Alongside improved faithfulness,
human evaluations indicate that the resulting explanations are generally less
redundant and of higher overall quality and helpfulness.
A stronger, task-based notion of explanation utility assesses whether
explanations help users achieve concrete goals, such as predicting model
behaviour (Hase and Bansal, 2020), appropriately accepting or rejecting AI
recommendations (Buçinca et al., 2020; Bansal et al., 2021), or improving
performance in AI-assisted decision-making (Buçinca et al., 2020; Bansal
et al., 2021; Hashemi Chaleshtori et al., 2024). Chapter 7 contributes to this
line of work through a user study examining the role of explanations and
input evidence in assisting users in validating AI decisions in fact-checking.
The results show that users generally regard input evidence as the most
reliable source, highlighting the need for future work on how evidence should
be presented and how explanations can be more effectively grounded in
it (Warren et al., 2026b).
Manual evaluation of chain-of-thought explanations. Even when
CoT reasoning improves answer accuracy, it can contain human-detectable
errors, such as incorrect intermediate steps (Nguyen et al., 2024). Manual
evaluation therefore examines whether these reasoning steps are individu-
ally correct, coherent, logically ordered, and sufficient to justify the final
answer (Lyu et al., 2023; Khalifa et al., 2023; Prasad et al., 2023; Lee and
Hockenmaier, 2025). Such issues may undermine users’ trust in CoTs and,
consequently, their utility (Rong et al., 2024; Hashemi Chaleshtori et al.,
2024).
15


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
Although Chapter 6 primarily focuses on the automatic meta-evaluation
of CoT faithfulness metrics, it also conducts a qualitative manual analysis to
examine whether improvements under these metrics produce human-visible
CoT changes. Interestingly, some metrics lead to visible CoT–answer consis-
tency, while others result in less noticeable textual changes. This suggests that
human-perceived plausibility and current proxy-based faithfulness need not
improve together, underscoring the importance of evaluating and improving
both.
1.1.4
Complex Reasoning Tasks and Dataset Settings
Complex reasoning tasks require models to coordinate multiple capabilities,
such as relating information across input components (Thorne et al., 2018;
Bowman et al., 2015), combining supplied evidence with background knowl-
edge (Mihaylov et al., 2018), performing multi-step logical inference (Liu
et al., 2020b), and resolving conflicts between external context and parametric
knowledge (Xie et al., 2024). These tasks provide informative testbeds for
explainability because a correct answer alone does not reveal whether the
model relied on the intended parts of the input, used relevant knowledge, or
followed a reasoning process aligned with human expectations. The datasets
used in this thesis span the task settings summarised in Table 1.1. Detailed
dataset setups and the corresponding explanation and evaluation protocols
are provided in the respective chapters.
Dataset selection in this thesis balances coverage of diverse reasoning
demands with the intended roles and strengths of each explanation form.
Although all three explanation forms are, in principle, applicable across
these tasks, highlight explanations (Chapter 2 and 3) are examined mainly
in settings with distinct input components, such as claims and evidence or
premises and hypotheses, where identifying influential tokens and interactions
across input parts is particularly informative. NLEs (Chapter 4, 5, and 7)
are studied across natural language inference, fact-checking, and question
answering tasks, where these free-text rationales can integrate background
knowledge and communicate information not explicitly stated in the input.
CoT (Chapter 6) is evaluated on scientific and logical question-answering tasks,
whose multi-step reasoning demands the model to make explicit reasoning
traces. Together, these datasets enable the thesis to study diverse explanation
forms and apply corresponding evaluation protocols, providing complementary
insights into model decision-making across different scenarios.

1.2
Scientific Contributions
1.2.1
Diagnostic Evaluation of Input Feature Explana-
tions
1.2.1.1
Paper 1: Evaluating Input Feature Explanations through
a Unified Diagnostic Evaluation Framework
Highlight explanations, which can be categorised into three forms, are a
popular explanation type for explaining a model’s decision-making process by
highlighting key input features, including individual tokens, token interactions,
and span interactions. However, previous work has mostly evaluated each
highlight explanation form in isolation, including individual tokens (Atanasova
et al., 2020a; DeYoung et al., 2020), token interactions (Janizek et al., 2021;
Tsang et al., 2020), and span interactions (Ray Choudhury et al., 2023); see
Fig. 1.3. Thus, such work provides limited practical guidance on how to
select among these different forms in application scenarios, where principled
explanation selection is important.
18


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
To address this gap, this work (i) proposes a unified diagnostic evalu-
ation framework to enable automatic, comprehensive, and rigorous
comparison of these three highlight explanation forms across a set of prop-
erties (Sun et al., 2025a). The evaluation spans four important aspects:
Faithfulness, Agreement with Human Annotations, Simulatability, and Com-
plexity. To enable a fair comparison, I introduce a dynamic threshold to adapt
the metrics by taking into account the variation in token numbers within each
explanation type. Based on this framework, (ii) extensive empirical anal-
ysis is conducted across three explanation techniques for each explanation
type, two complex reasoning tasks, and two language models. Experiments
show that explanation types have distinct strengths across various properties,
with span interactive explanations generally outperforming the others on most
aspects. While highlight tokens excel in Comprehensiveness, one aspect of
Faithfulness, a trade-off between low Comprehensiveness and high
Agreement with Human Annotations appears in span interactive
explanations. This encourages future work to further mitigate this tension
and optimise highlight explanations across all desired properties, possibly by
combining different explanation types.
1.2.1.2
Paper 2: Evaluation Framework for Highlight Explanations
of Context Utilisation in Language Models
Language models, despite storing substantial knowledge in their parameters,
are expected to consider and leverage information in the provided context,
especially in tasks such as retrieval-augmented generation, question answering,
and fact-checking (Lewis et al., 2020b; Thorne et al., 2018; Jin et al., 2024;
Monea et al., 2024).
However, previous work has mainly studied which
knowledge source the model uses when presented with different types of
context (Yu et al., 2023; Monea et al., 2024; Xie et al., 2024; Hagström
et al., 2025a). Such works do not examine whether highlight explanations,
i.e., important input segments, can accurately indicate the model’s context
utilisation behaviour, e.g., which context parts are used for the model’s answer.
Previous evaluation of highlight explanations usually focuses on proxies, such
as faithfulness (Atanasova et al., 2020a).
To address this gap, this work (i) proposes an evaluation frame-
work to examine explanations’ ability to indicate a model’s context
utilisation, especially regarding whether or not the model answers from
the provided context, and more granularly, from which context piece and
19


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
Figure 1.4: Examples of explanations with high and low utility in indicating
the model’s context utilisation behaviour. Left: With one context conflicting
with the model’s parametric knowledge (“Havana”), Explainer 1 correctly
indicates that “Accra” in the context is used for the answer, while Explainer
2 fails. Right: With two such contexts, Explainer 1 correctly indicates that
“Cleveland” in the second context is used for the answer, whereas Explainer
2 incorrectly attributes the answer to “Philadelphia” in the first context.
Explainer 1 shows better utility than Explainer 2 in both cases. Originally
published in Sun et al. (2026a).
from which precise span the model generates the answer (Sun et al., 2026a).
This framework synthesises realistic challenges in retrieval-augmented gen-
eration by exploring four context-usage scenarios, Conflicting, Irrelevant,
Double-Conflicting, and Mixed, which mimic retrieval-time challenges such as
knowledge conflicts, irrelevant noise, and multi-document competition. See
Fig. 1.4 for examples of explanations with high/low utility in Conflicting
and Double-Conflicting scenarios. Three complementary metrics are lever-
aged for evaluation: document-level attribution accuracy, simulatability, and
token-level attribution accuracy. The framework is designed in a way that
the model’s output answer serves as the gold-standard indicator of its con-
text utilisation. Besides the evaluation framework, I (ii) propose a new
mechanistic interpretability-based method, MechLight, which maps the
model’s context-usage signals carried by specific attention heads to highlight
explanations. Across evaluations on four datasets, five models, and four
explanation methods, MechLight outperforms the others in most cases. At
20


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
the same time, widely used techniques, such as gradient-based methods, are
poor indicators of the model’s context utilisation. Interestingly, I find that all
techniques suffer from positional bias and are sensitive to the length of the
provided context. This suggests that future work should design explainability
methods that better reveal model context utilisation and mitigate such bias.
1.2.2
Utilising Input Feature Explanations for Faithful
Natural Language Explanations
1.2.2.1
Paper 3: Graph-Guided Textual Explanation Generation
Framework
Natural language explanations (NLEs) are an important explanation form
for expressing a model’s rationale for a prediction in free-text form (Miller,
2019; Wiegreffe et al., 2021). Self-rationalisation, where the explanation is
generated together with the answer, is considered to achieve better alignment
between the explanation and the answer (Alvarez Melis and Jaakkola, 2018;
Marasovic et al., 2022). However, these explanations often suffer from faith-
fulness issues: they do not necessarily accurately reflect the model’s internal
reasoning (Kumar and Talukdar, 2020; Atanasova et al., 2023; Lyu et al.,
2024). While a range of previous work has focused on improving faithfulness
by resorting to external knowledge, refining prompts, or designing training
losses (Majumder et al., 2022; Marasovic et al., 2022; Chuang et al., 2026), it
remains underexplored how to design intrinsic explanation generation with
cues from model internals.
To address this gap, this paper (i) proposes G-TEx, a framework
that leverages highlight explanations as cues for faithful NLE gen-
eration (Yuan et al., 2025); see the pipeline in Fig. 1.5. Specifically, three
types of highlight explanations are extracted and separately encoded as graph
structures using a graph neural network. These graphs are then integrated
into the model, which is fine-tuned using human-written explanations as
reference. The intuition is that highlights, whose faithfulness can be easily
measured (Atanasova et al., 2020a; Sun et al., 2025a), can provide internal
cues that guide the model towards generating more faithful NLEs. Their
compactness may also encourage explanations that are more concise, less
redundant, and therefore more likely to be preferred by humans. Based
on this framework, (ii) multiple facets of the generated NLEs are
assessed, including faithfulness, similarity to human-written ex-
21


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
Figure 1.5: Overview of G-TEx, which consists of four key steps: (1) A base
model such as T5 is trained using the task-specific dataset for label prediction.
(2) Three types of highlight explanations are extracted from the trained model.
(3) Graph structure is constructed based on the highlight explanations. (4)
Graph structure is integrated into the model with a GNN layer, after which
the model is fine-tuned for label prediction and NLE generation. Originally
published in Yuan et al. (2025).
planations, and human judgements. Experiments on e-SNLI, ComVE,
and ECQA (Camburu et al., 2018; Wang et al., 2020; Aggarwal et al., 2021)
show that the resulting NLEs are more faithful than those produced from
the fine-tuning baseline, align more closely with human annotations and
receive slightly stronger human-preference ratings. However, the magnitude
of the improvement varies across datasets and highlight explanation types.
For e-SNLI (Camburu et al., 2018), where interactions between two input
parts are important, token and span interactive explanations yield larger
faithfulness gains. For ComVE (Wang et al., 2020), where the first input
component is fixed and the candidate statements contain the principal distinc-
tions, highlight token explanations perform best, suggesting that in this task
the interactions between different input parts play a smaller role in model’s
decision-making. Overall, highlight-guided NLE generation is a promising
direction, although its faithfulness benefits can be model- and task-dependent.
Future work should continue developing and refining the highlight-guided
methods.
1.2.2.2
Paper 4: Explaining Sources of Uncertainty in Automated
Fact-Checking
Fact-checkers highlight the need to assess source reliability and reconcile con-
flicting evidence in AI-assisted fact-checking (Warren et al., 2025; Schlichtkrull

The evidence in Evidence 1 that "SARS-CoV-2" aligns with the statement in the 
Claim "SARS-CoV-2", confirming the virus's identity. This agreement slightly 
reduces uncertainty due to the exact match.
The evidence in Evidence 1, "which is identical or similar in different species of 
animals such as pigs, ferrets, cats," aligns with the claim "cats can be infected 
with". This agreement strengthens the claim by indicating that cats are among the 
susceptible species, reducing uncertainty.
However, the statement in Evidence 1, "through domestic pets." conflicts with the 
statement in Evidence 2, "Notification of presumptive positive animal test results 
triggered a One Health* investigation by state and federal partners, who 
determined that no further transmission events to other animals or persons had 
occurred." This disagreement introduces significant uncertainty, as it suggests 
that while cats can be infected, there is no evidence of them spreading the virus 
further, contrary to the claim.
Natural language explanations of model uncertainty
conflicting 
evidence
entity match relevant 
to claim
Figure 1.6: An example of CLUE output for a claim with two evidence pas-
sages: model verdict and certainty, and an uncertainty explanation grounded
in conflict/agreement span interactions. Originally published in Sun et al.
(2026c).
et al., 2023), since in realistic fact-checking scenarios, multiple pieces of
evidence may be provided and may cause conflicts that affect model uncer-
tainty (Graves, 2017; Micallef et al., 2022). However, existing approaches
to communicating uncertainty usually rely on numerical confidence, such as
“I am 90% sure”, or verbal hedges, such as “I am not sure, but...”, which
cannot surface the sources of the model’s uncertainty (Kadavath et al., 2022;
Mielke et al., 2022; Yona et al., 2024). Meanwhile, previous explainability
work mainly explains the model’s verdict and neglects where its uncertainty
arises (Atanasova et al., 2020b; Zeng and Gao, 2024).
To fill this gap, this paper (i) proposes the first framework, CLUE, to
23


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
explain model uncertainty in natural language explanations, especially
by pinpointing agreement and/or contradictory span interactions between the
claim and multiple evidence pieces (Sun et al., 2026c). See Fig. 1.6 for an
example of an uncertainty explanation in this setup. Highlight explanations,
especially span interactive explanations, can naturally serve as a bridge
from the input to the model’s answer. Therefore, I build the uncertainty
explanation generation pipeline by first extracting the most salient span
interactions between the input claim and evidence pieces, and then leveraging
two methods to guide the model to generate explanations that refer to these
interactions and discuss how they affect model uncertainty, via few-shot
prompting (Zhao et al., 2024b) and attention steering (Zhang et al., 2024c).
To assess their faithfulness to the model’s uncertainty, I (ii) propose a new
metric by extending the popular counterfactual faithfulness test to
capture model uncertainty instead of verdict change (Atanasova et al.,
2023; Siegel et al., 2024). Specifically, I measure the correlation between the
model’s uncertainty shift and the explanation’s mention of the inserted word,
under the assumption that a faithful uncertainty explanation should reflect
important input words that lead to changes in model uncertainty. Through
experiments on two fact-checking datasets and multiple models, I show that
CLUE generates explanations that are more faithful to model uncertainty, less
redundant, and better aligned with the predicted verdict than a prompting
baseline.
Human evaluation further shows that CLUE explanations are
generally preferred across multiple aspects, including helpfulness. Interestingly,
the two CLUE variants exhibit a faithfulness–plausibility trade-off: the
steering-based variant achieves higher faithfulness by adhering more closely to
the extracted span interactions, whereas the prompting-based variant is rated
as more helpful. This suggests that fragmentary span interactions may omit
contextual information that humans consider important, motivating future
work to produce more coherent, human-understandable span interactions and
to mitigate this tension.
24


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
1.2.3
Evaluating Faithfulness and Utility of Free-Text
Explanations
1.2.3.1
Paper 5: Investigating the Interplay between Contextual
and Parametric CoT Faithfulness under Optimisation
The faithfulness of chain-of-thought (CoT) explanations has received increas-
ing attention (Turpin et al., 2023; Paul et al., 2024; Tutek et al., 2025; Zaman
and Srivastava, 2025). Existing metrics mainly follow two paradigms: con-
textual faithfulness, which evaluates a CoT by perturbing the input or CoT
trace (Turpin et al., 2023; Lanham et al., 2023), and parametric faithfulness,
which evaluates it by removing CoT-verbalised knowledge from model pa-
rameters (Tutek et al., 2025). Both paradigms then test whether the answer
changes as expected. Previous work has compared these paradigms only
for specific CoTs and found that they can assign different judgements to
the same CoTs (Zaman and Srivastava, 2026). It therefore remains unclear
whether optimising one faithfulness metric produces gains that transfer to
other metrics, despite growing work on faithfulness enhancement (Paul et al.,
2024; Swaroop et al., 2025; Jia et al., 2026). Consequently, there is still
limited practical evidence about whether current metrics capture the same
underlying notion of faithfulness (Parcalabescu and Frank, 2024; Zaman and
Srivastava, 2025).
To this end, this paper (i) proposes FaithMATE, a framework
that provides a direct interface for improving CoT faithfulness
towards a specific faithfulness metric and comparing whether, and
to what extent, faithfulness gains transfer across different metrics
and paradigms (Sun et al., 2026b). See Fig. 1.7 for the pipeline overview.
Specifically, CoTs are first sampled from the base model, and a target metric
is applied to select the most and least faithful CoTs, which are then used
to construct preference pairs for direct preference optimisation. Faithfulness
gain transfer is finally evaluated across multiple faithfulness metrics, including
both target and non-target metrics, to study how gains on target metrics
generalise to non-target metrics. Through (ii) a rigorous study on three
medium-sized language models, two question-answering datasets,
and six faithfulness metrics, I find that optimisation towards parametric
faithfulness generally brings more consistent gains across both paradigms.
Furthermore, within the contextual faithfulness paradigm, faithfulness im-
provements induced by different metrics do not usually transfer to each other,

find that optimising contextual faithfulness metrics usually improves CoT–
answer consistency and mitigates post-hoc rationalisation, while parametric
faithfulness brings milder visible CoT changes and sometimes makes CoTs
more explicitly grounded in task-relevant facts and knowledge. Overall, this
suggests that current metrics do not measure the same notion of faithfulness,
and thus evaluation using a single metric can be misleading. Instead, these
metrics need to be considered together for comprehensive evaluation.
1.2.3.2
Paper 6: Show Me the Evidence: Evaluating the Role of
Evidence and NLEs in AI-Supported Fact-Checking
The linguistically coherent and persuasive outputs generated by LLMs may
cause users to over-rely on model decisions or recommendations (Bender
et al., 2021; Pauli et al., 2025). Previous work has explored whether including
explanations and links to evidence can reduce users’ over-reliance (Kim et al.,
2025); however, little work has investigated experimental setups in which the
26


Chapter 1 | Executive Summary
Figure 1.8: Overview of study design and flow. Originally published in Warren
et al. (2026b).
evidence documents are made readily accessible to users. Moreover, regarding
the role of explanations, prior work has usually focused on verdict explanations
and has reported conflicting findings: explanations can sometimes support
more appropriate use of AI advice, but they can also increase over-reliance
(Vasconcelos et al., 2023; Bansal et al., 2021).
To bridge this gap, this paper conducts a user study to investigate
the roles of explanations and evidence in helping users validate
model answers in a fact-checking task, across varying explanation
types, model certainty, and model correctness (Warren et al., 2026b).
As indicated in Fig. 1.8, users are shown a target claim, the model’s verdict,
the model’s certainty, and one of three explanation conditions: a verdict
explanation, an uncertainty explanation, or no explanation. Users also have
access to two click-to-reveal evidence passages, and they are asked to decide
whether to accept the model’s verdict or decline it. In addition, users are also
asked to self-report which information sources they used to make their decision,
selecting from multiple presented sources, such as explanation, evidence, etc.
Based on this setup, analysis shows that evidence is the dominant
information source participants use when validating the model’s
answers: 64% of participants open both evidence passages for every claim,
and only three participants do not open any evidence passages. Although
participants also judge explanations to be useful, especially when the model’s
answer is correct or when the model is highly certain, there is no significant
usefulness difference between the two natural language explanation types.
Furthermore, higher self-reported reliance on the AI system is associated with
greater use of explanations, whereas lower self-reported reliance is associated
with greater use of evidence. Overall, the paper highlights the central role of
accessible evidence in AI-assisted fact-checking and motivates future work on
