to the complexity of the task.
The explanations generated by our CLUE framework are ranked as partici-
pants’ first preference 67-78% of the time, compared to 24-34% of the time
for PromptBaseline. The explanations generated by CLUE are rated as
most helpful, containing the least amount of redundant information,
highest coverage, consistency, and overall quality approximately
twice as often as those generated using PromptBaseline.
Although
both
CLUE-Span
and
CLUE-Span+Steering
outperform
168


Chapter 5 | Explaining Uncertainty in Automated Fact-Checking
PromptBaseline in automatic metrics (§5.4.5) and are ranked higher than
PromptBaseline in the human study (Table 5.11 in App. 5.8.9.2), we observe
a mild faithfulness-plausibility trade-off when comparing the two CLUE
variants. CLUE-Span+Steering achieves the highest automatic faithfulness,
whereas CLUE-Span is ranked slightly higher in human-perceived overall
quality.
A possible reason is that CLUE-Span+Steering adheres closely
to the top-k=3 span interactions (higher Span-Coverage and lower Span-
Extraneous), which improves faithfulness but can reduce fluency/naturalness
when some extracted spans are fragmentary. In contrast, CLUE-Span may
capture additional points that participants deemed important, but not
captured by the extracted span interactions (Ray Choudhury et al., 2023).
This pattern aligns with the well-documented trade-off between faithfulness
and plausibility (Agarwal et al., 2024; Atanasova et al., 2023; Lu and Ma,
2024), and calls for future work to enhance both aspects for improved NLE
generation, e.g., by improving the quality of the extracted span interactions.
5.6
Related Work
5.6.1
Uncertainty Quantification in LLMs
Recent work estimates LLM uncertainty primarily with white-box, logit-
derived measures: predictive entropy of answer distributions
(Kadavath
et al., 2022; Yang et al., 2025), aggregation across generations
(Malinin
and Gales, 2021), and response consistency via semantic similarity (Duan
et al., 2024; Kuhn et al., 2023; Nikitin et al., 2024). For closed-source models,
uncertainty is typically elicited through verbalised confidence (Lin et al., 2022;
Mielke et al., 2022), which is overconfident and unreliable (Yona et al., 2024;
Harsha Tanneru et al., 2024), or approximated by diversity under paraphrased
prompts (Zhang et al., 2024a; Chen and Mueller, 2024), which is compute-
intensive and conflates prompt noise with model uncertainty. Accordingly,
our method focuses on open-source models and adopts predictive entropy, a
simple, interpretable, efficient white-box metric computed from answer logits,
avoiding prompt-induced noise.
169


Chapter 5 | Explaining Uncertainty in Automated Fact-Checking
5.6.2
Linguistic Expressions of Uncertainty
Numerical uncertainty estimates are difficult for end-users to act upon as
they do not address sources of uncertainty (Warren et al., 2025). Linguistic
expressions of uncertainty, e.g., phrases such as “I’m sure” (Mielke et al., 2022;
Tian et al., 2023; Xiong et al., 2024; Ji et al., 2025; Farquhar et al., 2024;
Kim et al., 2024c), may be more intuitive to understand than numerical ones
(Zimmer, 1983; Wallsten et al., 1993; Windschitl and Wells, 1996). However,
they are not necessarily faithful reflections of the model’s uncertainty (Yona
et al., 2024; Harsha Tanneru et al., 2024) risking misleading users (Steyvers
et al., 2025). Moreover, they do not explain why the model is uncertain, as
our CLUE method does.
5.6.3
Generating Natural Language Explanations for
Fact-Checking
Natural language explanations (NLEs) justify model predictions for lay read-
ers in fact-checking (Wei Jie et al., 2024), complementing summarization
and social-media work that provides graphs, manipulated spans as ratio-
nales (Ribeiro et al., 2022; Chan et al., 2023; Huang et al., 2025). In fact-
checking, early systems extracted key sentences from supplied articles as
explanations (Atanasova et al., 2020b); later work improved NLE fluency via
post-editing (Jolly et al., 2022) and trained models on journalist-written fact
checks collected from fact-checking websites (Feher et al., 2025; Althabiti
et al., 2023). More recent methods use highlight-based explanations as signals
to enhance NLE quality, employing techniques such as graph-based mod-
eling (Yuan et al., 2025) and self-refinement (Wang and Atanasova, 2025).
Another line of work focuses on few-shot approaches that avoid model super-
vision, including prompting GPT-3 to produce evidence summaries (Brown
et al., 2020; Stammbach and Ash, 2020), inserting a planning step (Zhao
et al., 2024b), and leveraging retrieval-augmented language models (Zeng
and Gao, 2024). However, existing methods are often either unfaithful to
model processes (Atanasova et al., 2023; Siegel et al., 2024, 2025) or fail
to address model uncertainty (Warren et al., 2025), limiting their practical
utility in fact-checking (Schmitt et al., 2024). Recent work in a fact-checking
context has shown that explanations of model uncertainty were judged to
be more helpful than providing the model verdict and numerical uncertainty
alone (Warren et al., 2026b), suggesting that generating such explanations
170


Chapter 5 | Explaining Uncertainty in Automated Fact-Checking
may assist people to reason about the reliability of automated fact-checking
predictions. Our framework addresses these gaps by explicitly explaining
sources of uncertainty in automatic fact-checking, yielding explanations that
are both faithful to model uncertainty and provide actionable insights for
fact-checkers.
5.7
Conclusion
We present the first framework, CLUE, for generating NLEs of model un-
certainty by referring to the conflicts and agreements between claims and
multiple pieces of evidence in a fact-checking task. Our method, evaluated
across three language models and two datasets, demonstrates significant im-
provements in both faithfulness to model uncertainty and label consistency
compared to standard prompting. Evaluations by human participants further
demonstrate that the explanations generated by CLUE are more helpful, more
informative, less redundant, and more logically consistent with the input.
This work establishes a foundation for explainable fact-checking systems,
providing end users with grounded, faithful explanations that reflect the
model’s uncertainty. Furthermore, our approach may prove useful for future
work in a wide range of information-seeking and retrieval-augmented tasks
(e.g., question answering) in which explaining uncertainty given conflicting
context is critical.
Limitations
Our paper proposes a novel framework for generating NLEs towards the
model’s uncertainty by explicitly pointing to the conflicts or agreements
between the claim and multiple pieces of evidence. While our framework
demonstrates improved explanation quality through rigorous evaluation across
multiple language models and datasets, we acknowledge several limitations.
Our experiments are constrained to medium-sized models (Qwen2.5-
14B-Instruct, Gemma2-9B-It, OLMo-2-1124-13B-Instruct, and DeepSeek-R1-
Distill-Qwen-14B), which are selected based on computational limitations.
Although these models show significant improvements over baseline perfor-
mance, our results suggest that larger models (e.g., 70B parameter scale) may
further enhance explanation quality, particularly in terms of coverage and
171


Chapter 5 | Explaining Uncertainty in Automated Fact-Checking
redundancy. Our framework’s modular design readily accommodates such
scaling.
CLUE includes an auxiliary relation labeler Lrel that assigns each extracted
span interaction one of {agree, disagree, unrelated}. In the main
experiments we use GPT-4o for this step to reduce relation-label noise, but
this step is optional: Lrel is modular, and a fully open-weight variant (e.g.,
Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct) yields comparable downstream NLE quality with only
a small drop (App. 5.8.3.2). Reducing this remaining gap while remaining
fully open-weight may require improving relation-label prompting (more local
context, higher-quality shots) or lightly fine-tuning an open-weight model on
a compact relation-judgment set (App. 5.8.3.4).
Like most multi-stage pipelines, CLUE can occasionally propagate up-
stream errors: manual inspection reveals failure modes such as fragmentary
spans and occasional relation mislabels, which can in turn reduce NLE faith-
fulness (App. 5.8.3.3). These issues could be mitigated by encouraging more
complete semantic spans during extraction (e.g., mild constraints during
community detection), improving relation-label robustness (prompting or
lightweight fine-tuning), and adding lightweight generation-time checks that
down-weight unreliable span interactions before producing the final explana-
tion (App. 5.8.3.4).
In this study, we focus on the HealthVer and DRUID datasets, which
pair claims with discrete pieces of evidence, making them ideal for studying
evidence-conflict scenarios. Our experiments primarily focus on setups where
one claim and two or three pieces of evidence are presented. Although we
did not explore setups involving more complex claim-evidence interactions,
when multiple claims and/or pieces of evidence are introduced, our framework,
grounded in pairwise interactions, can seamlessly accommodate more complex
scenarios by simply increasing the number of input claim/evidence pieces; no
methodological changes are required.
CLUE targets settings where users need to understand why the model
is uncertain by grounding uncertainty in concrete claim-evidence and inter-
evidence conflicts/agreements. This focus is motivated by qualitative findings
that professional fact-checkers want systems to “show the work” behind a
decision, especially when evidence-based tensions drive uncertainty, so that
users can decide what to verify next and when to trust the output (Warren
et al., 2025). While in this paper, we conduct a human study and find
that uncertainty explanations generated by our proposed CLUE framework
are more helpful than those from the simple prompting method, we do not
172


Chapter 5 | Explaining Uncertainty in Automated Fact-Checking
study whether these uncertainty-oriented explanations are preferable in every
context compared to alternative methods for communicating uncertainty (e.g.,
numeric confidence (Zimmer, 1983; Wallsten et al., 1993; van der Waa et al.,
2020; Liu et al., 2020a) or verbal hedges (Lin et al., 2022; Mielke et al., 2022;
Yona et al., 2024; Kim et al., 2024c)). In-situ evaluation with professional fact-
checkers, potentially including interface-level ablation studies (e.g., tuning the
number of surfaced interactions k and using compact/expandable formats),
would be needed to study the effects of uncertainty explanations on efficiency,
error detection, and calibrated trust in other contexts.
Our work is limited to the scope of explaining model uncertainty arising
from evidence conflicts. While this captures a critical subset of cases, real-
world uncertainty may also stem from other sources, including insufficient
evidence, knowledge gaps in the model, and context-memory conflicts. We
view this work as a foundational step toward broader research on model
uncertainty explanation.
Ethical Considerations
This work concerns automated fact-checking, which aims to reduce the harm
and spread of misinformation, but nevertheless has the potential for harm or
misuse through model inaccuracy, hallucination, or deployment for censorship.
Our current work aims to provide explanations that allow users to examine the
outputs of these systems more critically, and so we do not see any immediate
risks associated with it.
Our work is limited to examining claims, evidence, and explanations in
English, and so our results may not be generalisable to other languages. As
the task involved complex reasoning about technical subjects, we screened
our participants to be native English speakers to ensure that they could fully
understand the material and increase the chances of high-quality responses
(see 5.8.9.1 for details). However, this criteria may also introduce or reinforce
existing biases and limit the generalisability of our findings. Participants were
informed about the study and its aims before agreeing to provide informed
consent. No personal data was collected from participants and they received
fair payment for their work (approximately 9 GBP/hour).
173


Chapter 5 | Explaining Uncertainty in Automated Fact-Checking

5.8.2
Method: Selecting attention heads to steer
Following Zhang et al. (2024c), we steer only a selected subset of attention
heads rather than all of them, because targeted steering yields larger gains in
output quality. Our selection criterion, however, differs from theirs: instead
174


Chapter 5 | Explaining Uncertainty in Automated Fact-Checking
of ranking heads by their impact on task accuracy, we rank them by how
strongly they affect the model’s predictive uncertainty during fact-checking.
Concretely, for each fact-checking dataset chosen in this work (see details
in §5.3.1), D, we draw a validation subset Dd with |Dd| = 300 examples. For
every input X ∈Dd, we compute the model’s baseline uncertainty score u(X)
when it predicts the fact-checking label as stated in §5.2.2. Then, for each
attention head identified by layer ℓand index h, we zero out that head, re-run
the model, and measure the absolute change in uncertainty
∆u(X, ℓ, h) =
 u(X) −u/o(l,h)(X)
.
Averaging ∆u(X, l, h) over all X ∈Dd yields a single importance score for
head (ℓ, h). We rank the heads by this score and keep the top t heads for
each dataset and each model. Note that we set t = 100 in line with the
recommendation of Zhang et al. (2024c) and to balance steering effectiveness
against the risk of degeneration.
5.8.3
Conflict and Agreement Identification Details
5.8.3.1
Prompt Example for Assigning Relation Labels to Cap-
tured Span Interactions
To identify agreements and conflicts between the claim and the two evidence
passages, we use the prompt in Figure 5.3 to label each extracted span
interaction (see §5.2.3).
5.8.3.2
Open-weight Alternative for Span-Interaction Relation
Labeling
This section studies how the choice of relation labeler affects downstream
NLE quality in CLUE. While we use GPT-4o as the default relation labeler
to reduce labeling noise, we show that a strong open-weight alternative is
also feasible, with only a small performance drop.
As discussed in Sec. 5.2.3, CLUE uses a relation labeler Lrel to assign
each extracted span interaction one of {agree, disagree, unrelated}. We
considered two labeler options: (i) a closed-API model, which can reduce
label noise at the cost of API usage, and (ii) an open-weight model, which is
fully local but may yield slightly noisier labels and thus introduce additional
noise in downstream NLE generation.

Table 5.4: Effect of span-interaction relation labeler choice. We rerun CLUE

drop in downstream NLE quality. Bold indicates the better result per metric
within each dataset.
These results indicate that an open-weight labeler is feasible in
CLUE, and the framework is not tied to a proprietary component.
In this work, we use GPT-4o in the main experiments because it provides
consistently stronger relation labeling and reduces the risk of error propagation
from noisy relation labels to the final explanations.
We further discuss
how upstream labeling errors can propagate and mitigation strategies in
App. 5.8.3.3.
A natural concern is whether using a closed-API labeler compromises
transparency. We emphasize that CLUE is white-box with respect to the
backbone model: uncertainty scoring, span-interaction extraction, and atten-
tion steering require access to the backbone model internals, which closed
APIs do not provide. Accordingly, running the entire pipeline with GPT-4o
would forgo these white-box signals and reduce the method to surface-level
prompting; prior work shows such verbalized uncertainty can be unreliable,
and fact-checking practice requires systems to “show the work” behind a
decision (Yona et al., 2024; Harsha Tanneru et al., 2024; Warren et al., 2025).
GPT-4o is used only for the narrow task of pairwise relation judgment
after span interactions have already been extracted from backbone internals;
as such, it does not affect the transparency of the core framework.
5.8.3.3
Error Propagation Analysis
We manually inspected 50 HealthVer instances (top-3 span pairs per instance;
N=150 span pairs) to assess (i) span extraction quality and (ii) relation
labeling accuracy when using GPT-4o as Lrel.
177


Chapter 5 | Explaining Uncertainty in Automated Fact-Checking
Span extraction quality.
Most extracted spans are very short: 105/150
span pairs contain two-token spans (often salient nouns such as subjects/ob-
jects). In 35/150 cases, at least one span is incomplete (e.g., “D” instead
of “vitamin D”) or semantically underspecified (e.g., “is not”). Overall, the
extractor tends to favor short, high-salience tokens, suggesting potential for
future work to encourage more complete semantic units.
Relation labelling accuracy.
On these 150 span pairs, GPT-4o achieves
86% accuracy (129/150 correct). Among the 21 errors, 12 are cases where
an agree relation is mislabelled as unrelated, typically when one span is
incomplete (e.g., “D” vs. “vitamin D”), indicating that span completeness is
a primary failure mode.
Propagation to downstream NLEs.
Using Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct with
CLUE-Span+Steering, 13/21 mislabeled interactions are explicitly mentioned
in the generated NLEs, showing that upstream errors can propagate into
explanations. Moreover, the subset of instances with mislabeled interac-
tions exhibits lower faithfulness (mean Entropy-CCT 0.020) than the overall
HealthVer–Qwen CLUE-Span+Steering average (0.033; Table 1), suggesting
that incorrect relation labels can reduce explanation faithfulness.
This analysis suggests that fragmentary spans and relation-label noise can
be an upstream error source and carry forward to the final NLEs, motivating
mitigation strategies discussed in App. 5.8.3.4 for future work.
5.8.3.4
Mitigating Error Propagation
We outline several directions to mitigate error propagation in future work:
(1) Improve span extraction: add mild constraints during community
detection so extracted spans more consistently capture complete semantic units
rather than clipped tokens. (2) Improve relation labeling robustness:
use stronger prompting (broader local context, higher-quality example shots)
or lightly fine-tune an open-weight model on a compact relation-judgment set.
(3) Generation-time sanity checks: add a lightweight consistency check
that flags or down-weights span pairs judged internally inconsistent with the
claim/evidence before producing the final NLE.
178


Chapter 5 | Explaining Uncertainty in Automated Fact-Checking

5.8.5.3
Memory requirement for steering 100 heads
For selecting the most important 100 attention heads (App. 5.8.2), the
peak VRAM is 29.8 GiB. For steering these heads during NLE generation
(CLUE-Span+Steering), the average peak VRAM per instance is 30.84 GiB.
Thus, a GPU with 40GB of memory satisfies the requirement for attention
steering across 100 heads.
5.8.5.4
GPT-4o API cost for relation labeling
When using GPT-4o as Lrel, the total relation-labeling cost scales linearly with
the number of labeled instances. In our main experiments, the cumulative
cost across datasets and backbones was approximately $30 (about $0.003 per
instance), with an average input length of ∼1k tokens and an average output
length of ∼50 tokens per call.
5.8.6
Methodology Supplement: Faithfulness Evalua-
tion Details
This supplement provides implementation and statistical details for the
Entropy-CCT faithfulness metric introduced in §5.4.1. We describe (i) how
counterfactual perturbations are generated, (ii) how Entropy-CCT relates
to the original CCT, and (iii) how we test statistical significance and report
results.
180


Chapter 5 | Explaining Uncertainty in Automated Fact-Checking
5.8.6.1
Entropy-CCT Perturbation Generation
To evaluate how faithfully each NLE reflects model uncertainty, we generate
multiple counterfactuals per instance, following Atanasova et al. (2020b) and
Siegel et al. (2024) (see §5.4.1). For every input, comprising one claim and
two evidence passages, we first tag part-of-speech with spaCy (Honnibal and
Montani, 2017) (en_core_web_sm), then choose four random insertion sites
(uniformly among tokens tagged as NOUN or VERB). At each site we insert
either (i) a random adjective before a noun or (ii) a random adverb before a
verb. The candidate modifiers are drawn uniformly from WordNet (Miller,
1992) using NLTK (Bird et al., 2009): we construct adjective/adverb candidate
lists by collecting all lemma names from all WordNet adjective/adverb synsets,
respectively. Because we sample three random candidates for each of the
four positions, this procedure yields 4 × 3 = 12 perturbations per instance,
providing a sufficient set for the subsequent Entropy-CCT evaluation, in
which we check whether the NLE mentions the inserted word and compute
the correlation between that mention and the uncertainty change induced by
each perturbation.
5.8.6.2
Entropy-CCT vs. Original CCT
CCT Siegel et al. (2024) evaluates faithfulness by perturbing the input and
measuring the resulting change in the model’s predicted label distribution.
Specifically, it uses the Total Variation Distance (TVD) between the pre- and
post-perturbation distributions P(· | X) and P(· | X′):
TVD(P, Q) = 1
2
X
i
|Pi −Qi|.
(5.12)
Entropy-CCT is a minimal adaptation of CCT that preserves the same
perturbation procedure and the same correlational test: we correlate the
perturbation impact with whether the inserted token is mentioned in the
NLE. The only change is the signal we correlate. Instead of TVD (probability-
shift), we use Absolute Entropy Change (AEC), i.e., the change in predictive
entropy, to directly target uncertainty (our object of explanation).
5.8.6.3
Statistical Testing & Significance
We test statistical significance of rpb using the standard Pearson correlation
t-test. Each perturbation constitutes one observation: for each dataset we
181


Chapter 5 | Explaining Uncertainty in Automated Fact-Checking
evaluate 600 instances with 12 perturbations per instance, yielding n = 600 ×
12 = 7,200 observations (App. 5.8.6.1). Because the point-biserial correlation
is algebraically equivalent to Pearson correlation between a continuous and a
binary variable, we use the standard Pearson t-test for H0 : rpb = 0.
Interpreting rpb and ∆rpb.
The Entropy-CCT score is the point-
biserial correlation (Tate, 1954) between the absolute entropy change |∆u|
and the binary mention flag m. Because it is mathematically identical to a
Pearson r computed between one continuous and one binary variable, it obeys
−1 ≤rpb ≤1. When rpb = 0, it means the high- and low-impact perturbations
are mentioned equally often. If the two strata are roughly balanced, every
+0.01 in rpb increases the probability that a truly uncertainty-influential token
is mentioned by about one percentage point (pp). A gain ∆rpb therefore
translates to an absolute improvement of ≈|∆rpb| × 100 pp in mention rate.
For instance, moving from −0.08 to +0.06 is a swing of 0.14, corresponding
to about 14 pp.
Significance testing. Because the point-biserial is a Pearson correlation,
the familiar t–test applies:
t = rpb
 
n −2
1 −r2
pb
,
(5.13)
t ∼t(n−2)
under H0 : rpb = 0.
(5.14)
With n = 7, 200 we have df = 7, 198; the critical two-sided values are
|t| > 1.96 for p < 0.05 and |t| > 2.58 for p < 0.01.
Faithfulness with significance results. Table 5.7 shows the point-
biserial coefficients rpb, which is our faithfulness measurement for model
uncertainty(See, Eq.5.9), the associated t statistics, and two-sided p values
for every model–method pair. Values that meet the stricter p < 0.01 criterion
are highlighted in bold.
Across both datasets and all three backbones, the PromptBaseline exhibits
negative correlations, implying a non-faithful tendency to highlight low-impact
tokens in the generated NLEs, with mean = −0.094. The prompt-only variant
of our CLUE framework CLUE-Span neutralises this bias and turns the average
into +0.027; two of its coefficients are clear p < 0.01, indicating a modest but
significant improvement regarding faithfulness.
The full CLUE-Span+Steering variant pushes the mean to +0.062 and
achieves p < 0.01 in four of six settings. Interpreting these numbers using

5.8.7
Prompt template for PromptBaseline, CLUE-Span
and CLUE-Span+Steering on Healthver and Druid
dataset
We designed two prompt templates for our experiments. The baseline prompt
(Figure 5.4) gives the model no span interactions; instead, it must first identify
the relevant agreements or conflicts and then discuss them in its explanation.
In contrast, the prompt used by our CLUE framework (Figure 5.5) supplies
the three pre-extracted span interactions (§5.2.3). The model is explicitly
instructed to base its explanation on these spans, ensuring that the rationale
remains grounded in the provided evidence.
5.8.7.1
Prompt template for PromptBaseline
To generate NLEs about model uncertainty without span-interaction guid-
ance, we craft a three-shot prompt that instructs the model to identify the
interactions most likely to affect its uncertainty and to explain how these
relations they represent affect it. (See Figure 5.4).
5.8.7.2
Prompt template for CLUE-Span and CLUE-Span+Steering
To generate NLEs about model uncertainty with the span-interaction guidance,
we craft a three-shot prompt that instructs the model to discuss how these
interactions, along with the relations they represent, affect its uncertainty.
(See Figure 5.5).

5.8.9
Human Evaluation Details
5.8.9.1
Participants and Materials
Participants
We recruited N=12 participants from Prolific, screened to
be native English speakers from Australia, Canada, Ireland, New Zealand,
the United Kingdom, and the United States. The study was approved by our
institution’s Research Ethics Committee (reference number anonymised).
Materials
Forty claims (20 from DRUID, 20 from HealthVer) were selected
at random for evaluation, while 120 explanations were evaluated in total.
For each instance, participants were provided with a claim, two evidence
documents, model verdict, model numerical certainty, and three alternative
explanations (see Figure 5.6 in 5.8.9.6).
The explanations presented to
participants were those generated using Qwen2.5-14b-instruct Yang et al.
(2024) based on its automatic evaluation performance.
Each participant evaluated explanations for 10 instances (5 labelled ‘True’,
5 labelled ‘False’), in addition to two attention check instances which were
used to screen responses for quality. To mitigate the difficulty and cognitive
demands of the task, we kept the number of instances per participant low,
and removed participants who failed attention checks from the data. In order
to minimise the risk of bias, each explanation presented to participants was
assigned a neutral label: ‘Explanation A’, ‘Explanation B’, ‘Explanation C’,
so that participants could not infer anything about how the explanations
were generated or indicate that one should be favoured over another. Each
explanation was also presented in a different, random order for each instance
(e.g., ABC for Claim 1, CAB for Claim 2, etc.) to mitigate order effects such
as primacy bias or recency bias. The order in which the response options
‘Explanation A’, ‘Explanation B’, ‘Explanation C’ also appeared in the same
random order.
188


Chapter 5 | Explaining Uncertainty in Automated Fact-Checking
Procedure
Participants read information about the study (see 5.8.9.3)
and provided informed consent (see 5.8.9.4) before reading detailed task
instructions and completing a practice example of the task (see 5.8.9.5).
Participants then progressed through the study at their own pace. The task
took approximately 20 minutes, and participants were paid £3 for their work.
5.8.9.2
Human Evaluation Results
Interrater agreement
In line with similar NLE evaluations carried out
by previous studies (e.g., Atanasova et al. (2020b)), interrater agreement
(Kendall’s W Kendall and Smith (1939)) was moderate to low (see Table
5.10). We attribute this to the relative complexity of the task and individual
differences in how the information was perceived.
CLUE Variant preferences
Table 5.11 shows the mean rank assigned
by participants to each explanation variant. The explanations generated
by CLUE were preferred by our participants to those generated using
PromptBaseline: the explanations generated by CLUE-Span+Steering
were rated as most helpful, highest coverage, and containing the
least amount of redundant information, while those from CLUE-Span
were judged to have the highest consistency and overall quality.We
observed slight variation between datasets: CLUE-Span+Steering tended to
be rated higher than CLUE-Span for DRUID, and vice versa for HealthVer.
This may arise from differences in length and complexity of the input: DRUID
evidence documents, retrieved from heterogeneous online sources and often
consisting of longer form new articles, may have benefited from attention
steering more than HealthVer evidence documents which consist of focused,
shorter extracts from scientific abstracts.

5.8.9.5
Evaluation Task Instructions
What do I have to do?
In this study you will see claims, an AI system’s prediction about whether
this claim is true or false, how certain the system is about its label, and the
corresponding evidence used to make the prediction. You will also see three
different explanations for why the AI system is certain or uncertain about
its prediction. These explanations are intended to help you decide how to
interpret the true/false prediction.
Your task is to evaluate the quality of the explanations provided, not
the credibility of the claims and evidence.
What information will I be shown?
You will be shown examples of claims, evidence document, verdicts and
explanations.
• A claim is some statement about the world. It may be true, false, or
somewhere in between.
• Additional information is typically necessary to verify the truthfulness of a
claim - this is referred to as evidence or evidence document. An evidence
document consists of one or several sentences extracted from an external
source for the particular claim. In this study, you will see two evidence
documents that have been retrieved for a claim. These evidence documents
may or may not agree with each other.
• Based on the available evidence, a verdict is reached regarding whether a
claim is true or false.
• Uncertainty often arises when evaluating the claim and evidence to reach
a verdict. Each verdict is accompanied by a numerical uncertainty score
which represents the AI system’s confidence that its predicted verdict is
correct.
• You will see 3 alternative explanations for where uncertainty arises with
regard to the verdict. Note that these explanations focus on the AI system’s
uncertainty, not the verdict itself.
• You are asked to evaluate the explanations according to 5 different proper-
ties. The properties are as follows:
193


Chapter 5 | Explaining Uncertainty in Automated Fact-Checking
Helpfulness. The explanation contains information that is helpful for
evaluating the claim and the fact check.
Coverage. The explanation contains important, salient information and
does not miss any important points that contribute to the fact check.
Non-redundancy. The explanation does not contain any information
that is redundant/repeated/not relevant to the claim and the fact check.
Consistency. The explanation does not contain any pieces of information
that are contradictory to the claim and the fact check.
Overall Quality. Rank the explanations by their overall quality.
• Please rank the explanations in descending order.
For example, you
should rank the explanation that you think is most helpful as ‘1’, and
the explanation that you think is least helpful as ‘3’. If two explanations
appear almost identical, you can assign them the same ranking, but as a
general rule, you should try rank them in hierarchical order.
• The three explanations, Explanation A, Explanation B, and Explanation
C, will appear in a different order throughout the study, so you may need
to pay some attention to which is which.
Important: Please only consider the provided information (claim, evi-
dence documents, and explanations) when evaluating explanations. Sometimes
you will be familiar with the claim, but we ask you to approach each claim as
new, whether or not you have seen it before. It doesn’t matter whether you
personally agree or disagree with the claim or evidence - we are asking you
to evaluate what the AI produces: if you were to see this claim for the first
time, would you find the explanation provided by the AI useful? On the next
page, you will see an example of the task.

Example of human evaluation setup
Here is an example of what you will see during the study. First, you will see
a Claim, and two pieces of Evidence, along with an AI system’s predicted
Verdict and the system’s Certainty that its prediction is correct.
The parts of the claim and evidence that are most important to
the AI system’s certainty are highlighted. Parts of the Claim are Red,
parts of Evidence 1 are Blue, and parts of Evidence 2 are Green.

Green).
Your task is to read the claim, evidence, and explanations, and rank each
explanation based on five properties.

Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
Abstract
Chain-of-Thought (CoT) faithfulness, i.e., whether CoTs genuinely reflect
large language models’ (LLM) underlying behavior, is typically evaluated
with metrics under two disjoint paradigms: contextual faithfulness, measured
by perturbing the input or CoT trace, and parametric faithfulness, assessed
by intervening on a model’s parametric knowledge. Yet prior work com-
pares them only descriptively. We fill this gap by proposing FaithMATE, a
unified preference-alignment interface for optimizing models towards either
faithfulness paradigm. It enables us to investigate the interplay between the
two paradigms, examining whether and to what extent faithfulness gains
generalize within and across paradigms. Across three models, two datasets,
and six faithfulness metrics, we find that the two paradigms are positively
coupled, yet asymmetric: optimizing towards parametric faithfulness yields
consistent gains across both paradigms, whereas the contextual counterpart
delivers more variable gains. Within the contextual paradigm, faithfulness
gains on one metric do not consistently transfer to others, implying that
existing contextual metrics capture disjoint facets of faithfulness and exposing
inherent trade-offs. These findings imply that CoT faithfulness is not a
monolithic objective and therefore requires multifaceted optimization and
evaluation.
6.1
Introduction
CoT elicits intermediate reasoning steps that have proven effective for im-
proving LLMs’ performance on various reasoning tasks Kojima et al. (2022);
Wei et al. (2022). Yet, questions have been raised about CoT faithfulness,
i.e., whether CoTs accurately reflect the model’s actual reasoning process.
In practice, CoTs can appear plausible to users while being weakly coupled
with a model’s internal computations Agarwal et al. (2024); Arcuschin et al.
(2025); Zaman and Srivastava (2026).
Existing work evaluates CoT faithfulness primarily through two paradigms.
Contextual faithfulness intervenes on the input and reasoning trace in various
ways, e.g., by inserting an error Lanham et al. (2023), or injecting answer
hints into the context Turpin et al. (2023); Chen et al. (2025b), and deems a
CoT faithful if the intervention is truly reflected. However, such measures
only consider contextual consistency instead of model internals. Parametric
199

Figure 6.1: Overview of our optimization-based meta-evaluation framework.
We sample CoTs from a base model, score them using contextual or parametric
faithfulness metrics, construct preference pairs, and optimize the model
towards selected faithfulness objectives. We then evaluate cross-metric transfer
across both faithfulness dimensions.
faithfulness, in contrast, intervenes on a model’s parameters rather than its
context: it deems a CoT step faithful if unlearning the knowledge underlying
that step changes the answer (Tutek et al., 2025). While this perspective
targets model internals directly, it is limited to the knowledge explicitly
verbalized by the CoT, neglecting information that shapes CoT generation
but remains unexpressed.
Although the two paradigms have largely been studied in isolation, they
can yield divergent faithfulness conclusions for the same CoTs Zaman and
Srivastava (2026), raising the question of whether they capture a shared
notion of faithfulness or reward different properties of CoTs. As methods
for improving CoT faithfulness proliferate, observational comparisons on
fixed CoTs cannot reveal what each faithfulness paradigm rewards under
optimization or whether and to what extent optimizing one transfers to the
other.
To this end, we introduce FaithMATE (Faithfulness Metric Alignment and
Transfer Evaluation), a unified framework for investigating CoT faithfulness
under optimization. It constructs faithfulness-targeted preference pairs for
optimizing contextual or parametric CoT faithfulness (Figure 6.1). This
200


Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
unified optimization interface enables investigation of the optimization-based
interplay between the two paradigms, while also allowing us to compare what
each rewards. Experiments on three models, two datasets, and six faithfulness
metrics reveal the following findings.
First, across faithfulness paradigms, contextual and parametric faithfulness
exhibit a positively coupled, yet asymmetric relationship (§6.5.1): optimizing
for parametric faithfulness delivers more consistent improvements across both
paradigms, while contextual optimization, although occasionally larger in
magnitude, is less consistent. This positions parametric faithfulness as the
more reliable target for broad generalization.
Second, within the contextual faithfulness paradigm, faithfulness gains
from optimizing one contextual metric do not consistently transfer to others,
e.g., between Adding Mistake and Paraphrasing (§6.5.2).
This suggests
that contextual faithfulness metrics are better understood as a family of
related but distinct properties, rather than interchangeable measurements
of a single latent quantity. Thus, we call attention to potential tensions
among contextual faithfulness metrics. Additional experiments show that
task-arithmetic merging of models, each optimized for a specific faithfulness
target, can partially mitigate this inconsistency and amplify the faithfulness
improvements.
Third, we qualitatively characterize what different metrics actually reward
under optimization (§6.5.4). We find that contextual metric-shared gains
are typically driven by improved alignment between reasoning and final
answer, whereas contextual metric-specific gains are mostly prompted by
the mitigation of post-hoc rationalization.2 By contrast, parametric gains
arise from more explicit grounding in task-relevant facts and knowledge. This
suggests that evaluating and reporting gains on a single faithfulness metric
can be misleading, as improvements may reflect distinct underlying behaviors
that are not interchangeable.
6.2
Related Work
CoT Faithfulness Metrics.
Prior work has mainly studied contextual
faithfulness, which intervenes on the CoT or input and checks whether the
intervention is reflected in the answer. Corruption-based methods perturb
2A model decides on the answer and then generates a plausible justification that explains
the answer rather than reflects how it actually arrived at it Turpin et al. (2023).
201


Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
the CoT, and measure whether the model’s answer changes (Lanham et al.,
2023; Zaman and Srivastava, 2025). Attribution-based methods measure
the alignment between input contributions to CoT generation and those to
answer prediction
(Parcalabescu and Frank, 2024; Admoni et al., 2026).
Another line of work uses Biasing Features, which inject answer hints and
check whether the CoT verbalizes them (Turpin et al., 2023; Chen et al.,
2025b; Chua and Evans, 2025; Young, 2026). Recent work further proposes
parametric faithfulness, which intervenes on a model’s parametric knowledge
rather than on the CoT. Tutek et al. (2025) unlearn the knowledge involved
in individual CoT steps from the model parameters and measure whether
the answer changes after unlearning. Given that contextual and parametric
metrics target different aspects of CoT faithfulness, we evaluate both families
within our unified FaithMATE framework under optimization.
Meta-Evaluation of CoT Faithfulness.
Parcalabescu and Frank (2024)
compare contextual faithfulness metrics and find that they often assign
divergent faithfulness labels to the same CoTs, suggesting that they are
better understood as measures of output-level self-consistency. Zaman and
Srivastava (2025) use model editing to construct faithful–unfaithful CoT pairs
and evaluate whether the target faithfulness metrics rank the faithful CoTs
higher. More recent work extends such comparisons to parametric faithfulness.
Zaman and Srivastava (2026) show that contextual and parametric faithfulness
metrics can yield different conclusions on the same CoTs. Such observational
comparisons, however, leave open how the two paradigms interact when
models are explicitly optimized for faithfulness. FaithMATE fills this gap with
a unified interface for optimizing either paradigm, enabling controlled study
of faithfulness improvement transfers across metrics and paradigms.
CoT Faithfulness Improvement.
Several methods have been proposed
to improve CoT faithfulness. Nevertheless, they are designed to optimize only
one specific type of faithfulness. FRODO trains the model to prefer correct
reasoning chains over counterfactual ones Paul et al. (2024). Li et al. (2025a)
use integrated-gradient attribution to recall question information missing from
the CoT, and weight sampled CoTs by their information gain when voting for
the final answer. FRIT optimizes towards faithful CoTs identified via causal
importance – whether interventions on reasoning steps change the answer
Swaroop et al. (2025). Höth et al. (2026) leverage differentiable attention
202


Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
manipulation to learn faithful reasoning through reinforcement learning. In
comparison, FaithMATE does not focus on better results in individual faithful-
ness metrics; rather, it serves as a universal preference optimization framework
applicable to both contextual and parametric faithfulness, facilitating the
study of how the two paradigms interact.
6.3
FaithMATE Framework
FaithMATE comprises four key steps: CoT sampling, faithfulness evaluation,
preference pair construction, and faithfulness optimization (Figure 6.1).
6.3.1
CoT Sampling
In the first step, for each data point x ∈D from a given dataset D, we
sample a set of m CoTs, denoted C = {c1, ..., cm}, potentially representing
varying levels of CoT faithfulness. A specific CoT ck comprising T steps
ck = (s1, ..., sT), is sampled from a model M (Table 6.5):
ck ∼M(· | x)
(6.1)
6.3.2
CoT Faithfulness Evaluation
For each CoT ck, we assess faithfulness using a suite of metrics F (§6.4.3). For
each optimization run, one specific metric fi ∈F is selected as the training-
time metric, i.e., Ftrain = {fi}, which is then used to rank preference pairs
(§6.3.3). The remaining metrics are treated as evaluation-only metrics,
Feval = F \ Ftrain.
6.3.3
Preference Pair Construction
For each input x and sampled CoT set C = {c1, ..., cm}, CoT faithfulness
preferences are ranked according to a specific training-time faithfulness metric
Ftrain (§6.3.2). The most faithful CoT is selected as the preferred response
203


Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
yw, while the least faithful CoT is selected as the rejected response yl 3:
yw = arg max
c∈C
Ftrain(c)
(6.2)
yl = arg min
c∈C
Ftrain(c)
(6.3)
6.3.4
CoT Faithfulness Optimization
We employ two offline learning strategies due to the high computational cost of
faithfulness metrics during inference: supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and direct
preference optimization (DPO) Rafailov et al. (2023).4 From Section 6.3.3,
we obtain training sets DSFT = {(xi, yi
w)}N
i=1 and DDPO = {(xi, yi
w, yi
l)}N
i=1 for
SFT and DPO, respectively, which are then used to optimize CoT faithfulness.
6.3.5
Faithfulness Gain Transfer
After optimizing the model towards fi, we evaluate it on Ftrain ∪Feval to mea-
sure direct gains on the target metric fi and transfer to non-target metrics. 5
Specifically, to assess how model optimization towards fi ∈Ftrain is
beneficial for improving faithfulness on the evaluation-only metrics fj ∈Feval,
we define faithfulness gain transfer:
δ(fi, fj) = fj(c
′) −fj(c)
(6.4)
where c ∼M(· | x) and c
′ ∼M
′
fi(· | x) are CoTs from the base model M
and the M
′
fi model optimized towards fi. δ ranges from -2 to +2, and a
positive score means optimizing fi can enhance a model’s faithfulness on fj.
Thus, a high δ indicates strong gain transfer between metric fi and fj.
3We use the strongest contrastive signal to facilitate effective DPO training.
4Detailed information about training strategies and training details are provided in
Appendix 6.8.1 and 6.8.3, respectively.
5We repeat this procedure for each feasible metric in F (§6.4.3), varying Feval with
optimization target to study the interplay among faithfulness metrics under optimization.

Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
6.4
Experimental Setup
6.4.1
Datasets
Consistent with prior CoT faithfulness evaluation studies Paul et al. (2024);
Tutek et al. (2025); Zaman and Srivastava (2025), we select two widely used
reasoning datasets (see examples in App. 6.8.2.1):
OpenBookQA
Mihaylov et al. (2018) is a question answering dataset
designed to test scientific reasoning. It contains multiple-choice questions
requiring the integration of provided “open book” science facts with broader
commonsense knowledge.
LogiQA
Liu et al. (2020b) is a dataset designed to evaluate logical reasoning
in natural language understanding. It consists of multiple-choice questions
that test various forms of reasoning, including categorical, conditional, and
disjunctive reasoning.
6.4.2
Models
In our experiments, we employ three widely used open-source instruction-
tuned models from distinct families with varying parameter sizes (Ap-
pendix 6.8.2.2): Gemma3-4B Team et al. (2025), Qwen2.5-7B Yang et al.
(2024) and Llama3.1-8B Grattafiori et al. (2024).
6.4.3
CoT Faithfulness Evaluation Metrics
We employ different metrics for contextual (§6.4.3.1) and parametric (§6.4.3.2)
faithfulness, respectively, following Tutek et al. (2025).
6.4.3.1
Contextual Faithfulness
Contextual faithfulness is assessed in the literature by examining reasoning–
answer consistency (§6.2). Commonly used Corruption-based and Attribution-
based contextual faithfulness metrics are included in this study.6
6We intentionally exclude Biasing Features (Turpin et al., 2023), as they are sensitive
to how hint acknowledgment is identified (Young, 2026).
205


Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
Corruption-based.
Lanham et al. (2023); Zaman and Srivastava (2025)
propose four approaches to corrupt CoTs: (i) Early Answering, which trun-
cates the CoT before the original answer is reached to elicit an early response;
(ii) Adding Mistake, which injects a mistake into one CoT step and regenerates
the remainder; (iii) Paraphrasing, which rephrases the first part of the CoT
while preserving the original meaning and regenerating the rest; (iv) Filler
Token, which replaces the CoT with meaningless symbols such as ellipses.
Faithful CoTs should lead to answer changes after corruption, except for (iii)
Paraphrasing, where the model is expected to derive the same answer. To
measure this change with greater granularity rather than treating faithfulness
as a binary property of the final answer Lanham et al. (2023), following
Zaman and Srivastava (2025), we employ the change in prediction score for
the top-predicted answer before and after corruption, denoted as ˆzi and ˆz
′
i,
respectively:
ˆzi =
exp(pθ(Li | x))
P
Lj∈L exp(pθ(Lj | x))
(6.5)
where L denotes the set of possible labels. The faithfulness score is then
calculated as (ˆzi −ˆz
′
i) ∈[−1, 1].7 Larger positive margins indicate a bigger
drop in the original answer’s probability after corruption, and, thus, a more
faithful CoT.
Attribution-based.
An intuition behind this line of approach is that a
truly consistent CoT should depend on the same key input tokens that drive
the prediction Parcalabescu and Frank (2024); Admoni et al. (2026). A
representative instantiation of this idea is CC-SHAP Parcalabescu and Frank
(2024)8, which uses Shapley values to measure each input token’s contribution
to the predicted answer and to the generated CoT separately, and then
computes the cosine similarity between the two contribution distributions.
This assesses how well the importance of inputs for the answer aligns with their
importance in the CoT, without requiring any modification or perturbation of
the model’s input. Higher consistency scores indicate greater CoT faithfulness,
with the score ranging from -1 to +1.
7For Paraphrasing, we use (ˆzi −ˆz
′
i). Appendix 6.8.4.1 provides details on the imple-
mentation of CoT corruptions.
8Due to exceptionally high computational cost, we only employ CC-SHAP as Feval
instead of Ftrain. See computational cost for all used metrics in App. 6.8.4.3.
206


Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
6.4.3.2
Parametric Faithfulness
In contrast, parametric faithfulness evaluates CoT faithfulness through in-
terventions on models’ parametric knowledge. Specifically, given an input x,
the target CoT c is first decomposed into T reasoning steps c = (s1, ..., sT).
ffhard provides a binary answer as to whether a CoT is faithful by measuring
whether unlearning the knowledge used by models in any CoT step causes
models to change their output prediction Tutek et al. (2025) (App. 6.8.4.2).
ffhard = I[∃si such that y̸ = y(i)∗]
(6.6)
where si is the i-th reasoning step, y(i)∗denotes the prediction made by
M(i)∗after unlearning knowledge involved in the i-th reasoning step, and I(·)
represents the indicator function.
To enable more fine-grained evaluation, we further propose ffcont, which
extends ffhard and calculates the proportion of reasoning steps for which
unlearning leads the model to change its prediction:
ffcont =
PT
i=1 I[si such that y̸ = y(i)∗]
T
(6.7)
ffcont is therefore a continuous signal ranging from 0 to 1, as opposed to ffhard.
A larger value indicates that more CoT steps can cause a model’s answer to
change, implying a more faithful CoT.
6.5
Results
We analyze two key questions: (1) whether a model’s faithfulness on one
specific metric is improved by optimizing towards another, i.e., faithfulness
gain transfer δ (§6.3.5), especially when they are from distinct faithfulness
paradigms (§6.5.1); and (2) whether this faithfulness gain transfer δ remains
positive across metrics within the contextual paradigm (§6.5.2). We also
examine how these trends vary across base models and training paradigms,
and how task performance is affected under faithfulness optimization 9(§6.5.3).
Finally, we conduct a qualitative analysis to examine what each faithfulness
paradigm enhances (§6.5.4).
9Direct target optimization can generally improve faithfulness on the matched metric,
supporting our framework as a reliable basis for cross-metric comparison (App. 6.8.5.1).

6.5.1
Faithfulness Gain Transfer Across Paradigms:
Parametric ⇔Contextual
We first investigate the faithfulness gain transfer δ between contextual and
parametric faithfulness (Figure 6.2; Table 6.13). Recall that δ > 0 means
positive faithfulness improvement on one metric after optimizing towards
another (§6.3.5).
Contextual ⇒Parametric.
Contextual faithfulness gains δ can positively
transfer to parametric faithfulness in 40/48 <model, contextual target, train-
ing paradigm, dataset> cases, indicating that optimizing contextual metrics
208


Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
can generally improve parametric faithfulness. Moreover, we compare each
contextual source against an upper bound obtained by directly optimizing
parametric faithfulness. On OpenBookQA, Filler Token and Early Answering
are the strongest (δ = +0.0399 and +0.0325), followed by Paraphrasing
(+0.0214) and Adding Mistake (+0.0101). Filler Token thus recovers roughly
83% of the direct-optimization gain, while weaker sources such as Adding
Mistake recover substantially less. This pattern holds on LogiQA: Filler
Token again emerges as the strongest source (δ = +0.0257, recovering about
79% of the direct-optimization gain), whereas Early Answering produces the
weakest transfer (δ = +0.0076, recovering only about 23%). These results
indicate that the magnitude of contextual-to-parametric gain transfer exhibits
high variance, and that certain contextual metrics can achieve parametric
faithfulness improvements close to those of direct parametric optimization.
Parametric ⇒Contextual.
The reverse direction yields more consistent
transfer gains. Optimizing for parametric faithfulness improves contextual
faithfulness in 24/30 <model, training paradigm, contextual metric> cases on
OpenBookQA and 23/25 cases on LogiQA.10 Parametric optimization transfers
most consistently to Early Answering and Filler Token, each improving
in all 11 <model, training paradigm, dataset> cases. Relative to direct
optimization, their cross-dataset average gains δ recover 82.9% and 85.7%
of the corresponding direct gains, respectively (Table 6.13). A plausible
explanation is that parametrically faithful CoTs are likely contextually faithful
as well (in roughly 95% of cases, App. 6.8.7). This suggests that parametric
optimization is a more reliable target for broad faithfulness generalization.
Parametric ⇔Contextual.
Overall, contextual and parametric faith-
fulness are positively coupled, yet asymmetric: (parametric ⇒contextual)
yields more consistent gains in contextual faithfulness, whereas (contextual ⇒
parametric) transfer is stronger per-metric but with greater variance across
metrics. From a practitioner’s standpoint, this asymmetry suggests parametric
faithfulness as the more generalizable optimization target.
10The Llama3.1-8B SFT configuration is excluded from LogiQA aggregation (see discus-
sion in §6.5.3).
209


Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
6.5.2
Faithfulness Gain Transfer Within the Contextual
Paradigm
6.5.2.1
Among Corruption-based Metrics
We first ask whether contextual faithfulness assessed by one metric can be
improved after optimization towards another within the corruption-based
contextual faithfulness paradigm. Figure 6.2 illustrates that optimizing one
of these metrics improves a single non-target metric in 45/72 and 51/72
<4 target metrics, 3 non-target metrics, 3 models, 2 training paradigms>
configurations on OpenBookQA and LogiQA, respectively, indicating non-
trivial within-paradigm transfer.
Asymmetry as training target (Ftrain).
The four contextual metrics
differ in how well they propagate gains to others. On OpenBookQA, when
employed as Ftrain, Paraphrasing and Filler Token are the most effective
training targets, with highest positive-transfer count of 14/18 and 12/18
<model, training paradigm, non-target contextual metrics> and mean δ
of +0.0125 and +0.0126, respectively (Table 6.13). Adding Mistake is the
weakest, transferring in only 7/18 cases. On LogiQA, Early Answering achieves
the largest mean transfer gain (13/18; mean δ = +0.0179), while Adding
Mistake has the highest positive-transfer count (14/18; mean δ = +0.0167).
Filler Token and Paraphrasing follow, each yielding a positive transfer gain
in 12/18 cases, with mean δ around +0.01. These distinct rankings suggest
that the extent of transfer within the contextual faithfulness paradigm largely
depends on the dataset.11
Asymmetry as evaluation target (Feval).
The asymmetry also appears
for Feval: Early Answering and Filler Token are the most stable receivers,
each improving in 28/36 <model, training paradigm, dataset, optimization-
target> cases when other contextual metrics are optimized. Paraphrasing
is least stable, improving in only 19/36 cases. The most asymmetric effect
appears between Adding Mistake and Paraphrasing: Paraphrasing decreases
in 8/12 (model, training paradigm, dataset) cases when Adding Mistake is
the optimization target, suggesting that the two metrics may be in tension,
11We hypothesize that for Adding Mistake, OpenBookQA’s reliance on associative factual
recall allows the model to bypass corrupted reasoning steps, limiting transfer to non-target
metrics.
210


Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
not orthogonal (Figure 6.5b). One plausible explanation is that Paraphrasing
rewards invariance under meaning-preserving reformulation, whereas the other
three all reward sensitivity to perturbations.12 Critically, these capabilities
may be asymmetric: invariance is foundational and supports sensitivity,
but training for sensitivity can actively harm invariance. Thus, contextual
faithfulness metrics share signal, but may not be interchangeable proxies for a
single underlying notion of faithfulness.
6.5.2.2
Between Corruption-based and Attribution-based Metrics
We next ask whether transfer holds across fundamentally different opera-
tionalizations of contextual faithfulness, by including CC-SHAP (§6.4.3.1), in
contrast to the corruption-based metrics studied above. Figure 6.2 reveals
that CC-SHAP can generally be improved by FaithMATE when optimizing
either contextual or parametric faithfulness. Prior work reports moderate
correlation between CC-SHAP and intervention-based contextual metrics
Parcalabescu and Frank (2024); our results extend this correspondence from
measurement-time correlation to optimization-time transfer. This provides
preliminary evidence that contextual faithfulness gains may transfer beyond a
single operationalization paradigm, at least for CC-SHAP.
6.5.3
Modulating Factors in Faithfulness Optimization
and Task Performance
Variation across base models.
The above trends hold qualitatively
across all three base models, but transfer rates differ substantially. Within-
paradigm transfer among the four corruption-based contextual faithfulness
metrics succeeds in 18/24 cases for Llama3.1-8B (75%), compared with 14/24
(58%) for Qwen2.5-7B and 13/24 (54%) for Gemma3-4B. The contextual-to-
parametric transfer is strongest for Llama3.1-8B (8/8 cases), followed by
Qwen2.5-7B and Gemma3-4B (7/8 cases each). This suggests that the same
contextual optimization signal can transfer differently depending on the base
model. The Llama-Gemma gap is partly confounded by scale, but Qwen and
Llama are size-matched yet differ by 17 points, indicating that scale alone
does not explain the variation. We leave other confounding factors that affect
faithfulness transfer, e.g., post-training recipe, to future work.
12Paraphrased CoTs largely maintain the semantic meaning of the original ones, so that
this is not an artifact of the Paraphrasing generator (App. 6.8.6.5).
211


Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
Training paradigm also matters.
Compared to DPO, SFT yields rather
limited faithfulness gains: averaged across all metrics, its mean improvement
is +0.013, about two-thirds of DPO’s +0.020, and occasionally harms faith-
fulness. The gap between SFT and DPO is most pronounced on LogiQA with
Llama3.1-8B. These results demonstrate that simply maximizing the likeli-
hood of highly faithful CoTs is insufficient to effectively improve faithfulness;
the contrastive signal provided by DPO, which distinguishes higher- from
lower-faithfulness CoTs, better guides the model towards faithful reasoning
Admoni et al. (2026).
Faithfulness optimization largely preserves accuracy, and often helps.
Table 6.11 shows that on OpenBookQA, task accuracy improves over the base
model in all configurations for both SFT and DPO, with SFT producing
larger average gain (+7.83% vs. +6.30%). On LogiQA, the picture is more
mixed (Table 6.12): accuracy improves in 17/30 configurations, with average
gains of +0.06% for SFT and +0.07% for DPO. A plausible explanation is
that LogiQA is more challenging, given the substantially lower base-model
accuracy. Overall, improving faithfulness tends to maintain or even boost
overall task performance.
6.5.4
Qualitative Analysis
Table 6.1 shows that, in the pre-alignment CoT, the model selects the correct
option yet gives reasoning that undermines the choice, suggesting reasoning-
answer inconsistency. By contrast, the post-alignment CoT explicitly anchors
its reasoning in the provided fact. Further inspection shows that contextual
faithfulness improvements arise primarily from improved reasoning-answer
consistency and mitigation of post-hoc rationalization (App. 6.8.6). Most
enhancements are shared across multiple metrics and are substantially at-
tributed to better reasoning-answer consistency. Metric-specific gains instead
reflect reduced post-hoc rationalization.
A plausible explanation is that
reasoning-answer consistency is foundational to contextual faithfulness, so
improving it yields broad gains across all metrics, whereas mitigating post-hoc
rationalization addresses more localized reasoning deficiencies captured only
by metrics sensitive to those phenomena. By contrast, the parametric top-shift
cases in Table 6.17 exhibit a less uniform surface signature: some become
more explicitly grounded in the provided fact, while others show only modest
visible CoT change. Their minimal overlap with the contextual top-shift cases

suggests that contextual and parametric faithfulness track different aspects of

6.6
Does Model Merging Help?
Motivation and Setup.
Optimizing towards a single contextual faith-
fulness metric yields gains that do not consistently transfer across other
contextual metrics (§6.5.2), suggesting that each metric captures a comple-
mentary and partial notion of faithfulness. This naturally motivates model
merging, which can combine the parameters of multiple specialized models
into a single model without additional training and potentially integrate their
complementary strengths Tang et al. (2024); Song and Zheng (2026). We
therefore merge contextual-faithfulness-optimized model pairs using the task
arithmetic method Ilharco et al. (2023). As we employ LoRA to optimize
the model (Appendix 6.8.3), each LoRA adapter represents a task vector τ,
defined as (θDPO −θbase). Model merging can then be achieved as task vector

Results and Discussion.
Table 6.2 shows that merging generally improves
over individual models, suggesting that task vector combination captures
synergistic interactions between distinct faithfulness objectives.14 Combining
Early Answering with Filler Token yields the largest gains, as both outper-
form the base model, leading to an amplified enhancement after merging.
Additionally, their task vectors exhibit moderate similarity (Figure 6.5a). By
contrast, merges involving Paraphrasing can underperform their components,
sometimes even the base model, and dilute gains on other faithfulness met-
rics.15 Thus, while model merging substantially benefits contextual faithfulness
on most metrics, it degrades faithfulness on Paraphrasing.
14Merging results on other models appear in App. 6.8.8.1.
15Based on the PCA projection of task vectors (Figure 6.5b), we hypothesize that
Paraphrasing may learn a different direction of parameter change compared to the other
metrics, so that merging acts more like subtraction than addition.

Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
6.7
Conclusion
We presented FaithMATE, a unified framework for CoT faithfulness opti-
mization that enables a controlled investigation of the connection between
contextual and parametric faithfulness. Across three models, two datasets, and
six metrics, three findings surface. First, the paradigms are positively coupled,
yet asymmetric: parametric optimization yields consistent gains across both
paradigms, whereas contextual optimization produces uneven improvements,
making parametric faithfulness a more reliable target for broad generalization.
Second, contextual metrics are internally inconsistent: optimizing a model
towards one contextual metric does not always yield improvements on the
others. Third, the two paradigms act through largely disjoint mechanisms:
contextual faithfulness gains stem from improved reasoning–answer consis-
tency and reduced post-hoc rationalization, while parametric faithfulness
gains stem from explicit factual grounding. We hope FaithMATE serves as a
foundation for developing faithfulness objectives that are both parametrically
grounded and contextually robust, and enables more precisely scoped claims
about individual faithfulness metrics.
215


Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
Limitations
We acknowledge several limitations, stated as follows.
Lack of User Study.
Assessing CoT faithfulness through human evaluation
presupposes that humans can meaningfully judge whether a CoT accurately
reflects a model’s internal reasoning process. However, this assumption breaks
down when we consider the opacity of modern LLMs. With billions of pa-
rameters forming intricate webs of interactions that transform input into
output, the actual computational pathways remain fundamentally opaque to
human observers. We lack the tools and understanding necessary to trace
how these vast parameter networks collectively arrive at any given prediction.
Consequently, human evaluation of CoT faithfulness, while intuitively appeal-
ing, may be methodologically flawed given our current limited insight into
LLM internals, consistent with Jacovi and Goldberg (2020); Parcalabescu
and Frank (2024).
Online vs. Offline Learning.
Given the high inference time required
for almost all faithfulness metrics, as indicated in Section 6.3.4, only offline
learning is feasible in our setting. Consequently, online-learning approaches
such as GRPO Shao et al. (2024) are not applicable. Setting aside compute
cost, multiple faithfulness metrics can be aggregated into the reward signal
used by GRPO, potentially enabling multi-objective faithfulness optimization.
Though worth exploring, it is currently out of the scope of this paper.
Scope of Experiments.
We follow previous work Tutek et al. (2025);
Zaman and Srivastava (2025) and focus on MCQA datasets, as this setup
relies on model answers being options.
Therefore, it provides a natural
testbed for the existing faithfulness metrics where observable answer changes
are required.
Given that it is generally underexplored how to measure
faithfulness for open-ended tasks, applying these metrics to such tasks would
be non-trivial. Moreover, the parametric faithfulness framework applies an
unlearning intervention designed to reduce the likelihood of an information-
bearing content word in the target CoT step (Tutek et al., 2025). In code and
mathematical CoTs, however, numbers and operators are generic, reusable
symbols; suppressing them therefore does not reliably erase the step-specific
knowledge and may instead disrupt the same operations in unrelated examples,
216


Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
making the metric difficult to reliably apply to these tasks. Taken together,
the limitations of the existing metrics constrain the task selection of our meta-
evaluation framework. Should more advanced faithfulness metrics become
available, our framework could be easily extended to these tasks. While we
focus on English, extending the evaluation to low-resource languages would
introduce additional confounders. For example, Zhao et al. (2026) found that
low-resource languages tend to exhibit higher faithfulness. In this regard, how
different faithfulness metrics behave across languages is worth exploring as a
separate study.
Inherent limitations of the faithfulness metrics.
There is currently
no gold metric for measuring faithfulness in the absence of a ground truth
for the model’s underlying reasoning. Therefore, the existing metrics are
used as proxies for faithfulness. In this study, we do not claim to verify their
validity, but to measure to what extent they agree under model optimization.
FaithMATE contributes by making the relationships among these proxies
explicit, especially regarding where gains transfer, and why multi-metric
evaluation is necessary.
A mechanistic study of the model’s underlying
reasoning behavior, for example, by examining its parameters and token
embeddings, could provide interesting insights into faithfulness measurement
but would constitute a substantial, independent line of work.
Ethical Considerations
Use of AI Assistants.
The authors used Claude to assist with language
polishing, including grammar, clarity, and coherence, as well as minor code
implementation support. All technical contributions, experimental design
choices, and final decisions were made by the authors.

Direct Preference Optimization.

6.8.4.2
Parametric Faithfulness Implementation
For the parametric faithfulness metric, we follow Tutek et al. (2025) and
implement FUR with NPO+KL unlearning. We segment each generated CoT
into reasoning steps, unlearn one step at a time with KL regularization on

a retain set sampled from other CoT steps in the same dataset, and then
measure whether the model’s answer changes.
Concretely, let DFG and DRT denote the forget and retain sets, respec-
tively, and let πref denote the fixed reference policy. The negative preference
optimization loss is defined as follows:
LNPO,β(θ) = 2
β E(x,y)∼DFG
"
log
 
1+
 
πθ(y | x)
πref(y | x)
!β!#
.
(6.13)
and the retain-set regularizer is defined as follows:
KRT(θ) = Ex∼DRT [DKL (πθ(· | x) ∥πref(· | x))] .
(6.14)
The complete NPO+KL objective used for step-level unlearning is defined
as follows:
LNPO+KL(θ) = λNPOLNPO,β(θ) + λKLKRT(θ).
(6.15)
222


Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
The NPO term discourages the model from assigning high probability
to the forget targets, while the KL term constrains the model to retain
its original predictive distribution on unrelated retain contexts. We use
the default NPO+KL hyperparameters from Tutek et al. (2025): β = 0.1,
npo_coeff=1.0, KL_coeff=1.0, ref_policy=fine_tuned, 5 training epochs,
and no warmup. We also follow their implementation in restricting the forget
and retain targets to content-bearing tokens from the selected CoT steps,
and in excluding steps without enough content tokens for a meaningful
intervention.
All CoTs are generated and evaluated in greedy decoding mode, i.e.,
without sampling in the evaluation phase.
6.8.4.3
Evaluation Cost
The faithfulness metrics differ substantially in their computational require-
ments. Early Answering and Filler Token each require one target-model call
per CoT for answer regeneration. Adding Mistake and Paraphrasing each
require two calls: one helper-model call to construct the corrupted CoT and
one target-model call to regenerate the answer.
For a CoT containing T reasoning steps, the parametric metric performs T
independent step-level unlearning procedures, each optimized for five epochs,
followed by T answer-regeneration calls. This distinction treats the five epochs
as optimization iterations within each step-level intervention rather than as
five independent unlearning experiments.
Table 6.10 reports average wall-clock evaluation time per instance on an
A100, averaged across datasets and models. The parametric metric is therefore
substantially more expensive than the contextual metrics because it requires
a separate unlearning intervention for every reasoning step. Developing more
efficient parametric-faithfulness proxies is an important direction for future
work.

our setting, preference-based optimization more reliably converts faithfulness
metrics into training signal than SFT does.

6.8.5.3

We calculate the bootstrap confidence intervals for the faithfulness
transfer results. For an optimization target fi and evaluation metric fj, we
first compute the per-instance transfer δn(fi, fj) (6.4), where cn and c′
n are
generated by the base and optimized models, respectively, for the same test
instance n.
For each bootstrap sample, we draw N paired instance indices with
replacement from the original test set and recompute the mean transfer delta
over the selected indices. We repeat this procedure 1,000 times and report

6.8.6
Pre- and Post-alignment CoT Example Analysis
To understand what drives faithfulness-score improvements after DPO train-
ing, we manually inspect CoTs with the largest per-CoT faithfulness-score
shifts, defined as ∆F(c) = F(cafter) −F(cbefore), where F denotes the applied
faithfulness metric. For each faithfulness metric, we pool all CoT–variant pairs
across four DPO variants of Qwen2.5-7B on OpenBookQA test set (n = 100),
and select the top-20 entries with the largest ∆F(c).17 This yields 80 top-shift
entries across four metrics, corresponding to 27 unique question instances.
We call a CoT metric-shared if it appears in the top-20 list of two or more
metrics, and metric-unique if it appears for only one metric. Among the 27
unique CoTs, 15 are shared and 12 are metric-unique: 2 for Early Answering,
17This top-shift selection keeps the manual analysis tractable while targeting cases where
faithfulness optimization produces the clearest observable changes.
229


Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
8 for Adding Mistake and 2 for Paraphrasing. The absence of Filler Token-
unique instances suggests that large Filler Token gains usually co-occur with
improvements captured by other metrics.
Table 6.16 presents representative examples. The cases illustrate three
recurring mechanisms: improved reasoning–answer consistency, post-hoc ra-
tionalization, and reasoning condensation.
6.8.6.1
Contextual Pattern
Shared multi-metric faithfulness shifts.
Metric-shared CoTs are cases
where faithfulness scores improve substantially under multiple metrics.
Through manual inspection, we find that these gains often come from
improved reasoning–answer consistency: the original CoT and final answer
do not fully support each other, while the post-alignment CoT better aligns
the reasoning with the selected answer. Despite reasoning that partially
supports the correct answer, 4 of 15 metric-shared CoTs reach incorrect
answers, compared with an overall base accuracy of approximately 89%. The
first two rows of Table 6.16 illustrate this pattern. In the first case, the
post-alignment CoT aligns the reasoning with the correct answer. In the
second case, the post-alignment CoT makes the reasoning more consistent
with the selected answer, but the selected answer remains incorrect. Thus,
large faithfulness-score gains can reflect better reasoning–answer consistency.
Metric-unique faithfulness shifts.
Metric-unique CoTs reveal more local-
ized effects: (i) Early Answering, both unique cases show post-hoc rationaliza-
tion: the pre-alignment CoT states answer-relevant information early, while
the post-alignment CoT evaluates the options before committing to the answer.
The third row of Table 6.16 illustrates this behavior: the pre-alignment CoT
is not logically wrong, but it reveals the decisive inheritance mechanism before
the option-by-option analysis, which Early Answering penalizes. (ii) Adding
Mistake has the most metric-unique instances. All 8 Adding Mistake-unique
cases originally contain redundant elaboration, though their answers are all
correct. DPO mainly condenses these CoTs, reducing the mean CoT length
(from 899 to 730 characters) while preserving the core reasoning chain. The
answers of all 8 cases remain correct after DPO, suggesting that the removed
content is generally not essential for reaching the correct answer. The fourth
row of Table 6.16 illustrates this pattern: the post-alignment CoT tightens the
reasoning traces and removes redundant clarifications. (iii) For Paraphrasing,

Chapter 6 | Interplay between Contextual and Parametric CoT Faithfulness
two metric-unique cases come with modest faithfulness improvement (+0.08
and +0.06), suggesting there are lexical-level changes rather than substantive
reasoning repair in the CoTs after DPO training. For Filler Token, every Filler
Token top-shift instance also appears in at least one other metric’s top-20 list.
This suggests that large Filler Token improvements tend to co-occur with
above mentioned structural changes, which can be captured by other metrics.
6.8.6.2
Parametric pattern
We next examine the parametric faithfulness top-shift cases. Compared with
the contextual top-20 lists, the overlap is minimal: the parametric top-20
shares only 2 cases with Early Answering, 1 with Adding Mistake, 2 with Filler
Token, and 3 with Paraphrasing. This is much lower than the overlap among
the contextual metrics themselves, suggesting that parametric improvements
surface substantially different cases.
At the same time, several parametric top-shift cases show visible CoT
improvements. A recurring surface tendency is more explicit option-based
reasoning grounded in the provided fact, although this tendency is milder and
less uniform than in the contextually faithful CoT case study. Table 6.17
presents three representative parametric top-shift cases: one with only modest
visible change despite a large parametric gain, one with clearer fact-to-option
grounding, and one with more explicit option-by-option framing. Although
some of these surface edits resemble contextual improvements, the overlap
between the selected instances remains minimal, suggesting that similar-
looking CoT revisions can arise from different underlying mechanisms.
This interpretation also resonates with prior work on parametric faithful-
ness. Tutek et al. (2025) show that parametric faithfulness is only weakly
aligned with human judgments of supportiveness, suggesting that internal
reliance on reasoning steps and human-perceived plausibility are distinct prop-
erties.
6.8.6.3
Summary
This qualitative case study suggests different surface signatures for contextual
and parametric faithfulness improvements. For contextual metrics, large gains
are often associated with clearer reasoning–answer consistency, reduced post-
hoc rationalization, or more concise reasoning. For parametric faithfulness,
the surface signature is milder and less uniform: some top-shift cases show only
